#2487. Access, Not Chances: How Como Took Manchester United Apart – LC

von Next Generation am 24.10.2026 in den Kategorien Champions League,Uncategorized mit 0 Kommentaren

Como beat Manchester United 4-1 — and in this case the scoreline really is a fair expression of the game. For long stretches the match was one-sided, not in terms of chance volume but in terms of access: Como dictated in every phase where the ball went, who received it, and under what conditions it had to be played on. United never got on terms in any of those phases.

Cesc Fàbregas’s side showed two things that rarely go together. Out of possession, a pressing structure with clear direction, isolation of one side, and man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress while everything around it was already speculating on the second ball. And in possession, a positional game of remarkable technical maturity that ties opponents in, switches play, and works systematically into the space behind them. The 4-1 is therefore less an outlier than evidence of work that has been built up over recent seasons.

This analysis goes through the phases one by one: first Como in possession and United’s response, then the work against the ball from the high press to defending the box, and finally the goals.

In build-up, Como moved away from their nominal 4-2-3-1 and sat closer to a 4-3-3, with the eights as the real variable: very free in their movement and above all active in the half-spaces. The base structure stayed stable — a flat four plus a six in front of them, sometimes ahead of the line, sometimes slightly behind the forward line.

Close to their own goal, Como consistently involved goalkeeper Butez as an additional central player in the build-up. That created a five-against-four against United’s front line — awkward on paper, more awkward still in execution, because Como didn’t use the overload to play through but to tie opponents in. From there, the same shape kept appearing on both the right and the left: centre-back, full-back, a player from the half-position in the eight space, and a six alongside. A diamond that wasn’t an end in itself but had a purpose: the diagonal route out.

Como’s build-up structure. From a 4-1 (flat four), the half-positions with Paz and Perrone are added. Small triangles and diamonds emerge. The three attackers ahead of them create additional options in behind.

One detail deserves particular attention. At times the full-backs stood not wide but flat, in a half-position. That sounds like a nuance, but it had a clear function: it vacated the wing and created the space the eights could drop into — Paz and Perrone did so regularly. If you don’t occupy the wing yourself, you force the opponent to guard it anyway.

ManU got no access near the ball and left large diagonal spaces open on the far side.

For United’s midfielders this was close to unsolvable. Tielemans, Mount and Andrey Santos spent the whole game struggling to establish any responsibility at all, let alone get into duels. When an eight dropped out, they couldn’t follow, and if one of them was tied in by it, a two-against-one opened up on the far wing. Como didn’t create that situation once; they created it serially.

The decisive mechanism lay in receiving and relaying the ball. The player in the deeper position usually worked with two touches: the first took the ball backwards, the second relayed it to the free teammate. At the end of every one of those actions stood a player with the game in front of him — and from that open position he could switch play.

What made it possible were the preparatory movements. Paz and captain Da Cunha first lured their markers in the wrong direction and then worked with the head start they’d bought. That head start isn’t only space, it’s above all time: time to control the ball first and relay it second, rather than having to do both in one touch.

A scene in the 17th minute is typical. Mason Mount steps out, Mazraoui goes into the duel at the same time — nominally United are in an overload here. Only neither of them really gets to the opponent, because both are too late. Paz can therefore calmly take his touch backwards and switch play directly with the second, dissolving the pressure before it had even formed.

When the build-up ran down the right, it showed in a recurring pattern: Paz and Da Cunha took the first touch backwards or inside and then switched with the left foot out of a closed body position. The players they found were systematically in open positions — Milla, the far-side six, or the wing-to-wing connection directly.

In the second half, Como adjusted the shape and more often played with two sixes, to form the diamonds faster and switch to the far-side six faster. Couto stood out in this: the right-back repeatedly played the chip into the six or ten space — six or seven times in a row during one spell. It was mainly Paz who received the ball there and could launch the attack.

In the second half Como focus on those diagonal spaces and look for them mainly with chipped balls.

Especially striking inside the diamond are the rotations between the players. Because of the connections between them, they moved in rotation depending on running path, space and ball movement. The same recurring spaces were re-occupied. Under pressure on the ball carrier, the outer players pushed flat or ran in behind the opponents; the highest player in the diamond burst out of the cover shadow and became the reference point in depth. On a deep pass, the two wide players released and joined the depth, able to offer themselves again immediately in an open body position. If the pass went via the flat wide players, the spaces in front of the new ball carrier could be run into. Every rotation, every positional adjustment was coordinated with the others and, in the end, produced a stream of pressure-resolving passes while Manchester United chased the press in vain.

Como bridged the middle third exceptionally quickly, via dribbles at speed and diagonal passes. Behind it stood a recognisable principle: first tie the opponent to one wing, then get behind them via the diagonal, and in the final third do the same thing again. In sum, that produced the shape of a Z — build-up on the right, transition on the left, finish from the right again.

In this example Como pin ManU in the press with their short passing. Thanks to the extra attacker on the ball-near side they can play through the pressure and switch.

The connections between wingers and striker carried these attacks and were deliberately kept flexible. The wide players sometimes stayed wide, sometimes came short, sometimes tucked inside. Douvikas accordingly either waited deep in front of the line for the chipped ball or, when space opened up on the wing, received the deeper pass and then ran in behind along the wing as soon as the ball sat on the full-back’s open foot. That flexibility produced the overloads on one side, and the overloads produced the central overload.

In the final third one element was added that is rare in fast transition teams: patience. Como drew opponents towards them with dribbles and short passes, used back-passes when needed, and then switched in front of the block. Da Cunha, Baturina and Paz were the three who could tie opponents in and then find the diagonal. They created the positions on the diagonal, were available to receive, and used their space to apply pressure. The block was usually tied in with short touches, the switch and the position behind the opponent prepared, and quick finishes sought.

The intensity in possession shouldn’t be underestimated. Practically every attack involved at least three or four players, the width was occupied, the centre likewise, and there were multiple options throughout. What they looked for almost every time was the space behind the opponent on the far side — exactly the zone that is closed last when a block shifts across.

Paz brought the right profile for this pattern. A strong left foot cutting inside from the right, he repeatedly dropped into the right-hand blind side of his opponents, got shots away there with few touches, and racked up the most shots of the match. That the finishes generally came off few touches isn’t a detail but the consequence of the time won further back.

As the move continues, Como again look for the blind side of the defenders. Here Douvikas and Paz are positioned in behind, with Da Cunha joining in front of the back line. After briefly pinning the opponents, the switch reaches Da Cunha, who misses the target from distance.

What carries all of this is the technical execution. Soft passes, sharp ones when the moment called for it, consistently well timed and played into the right foot — for ninety minutes. Passing that secure at that tempo is what makes a variable concept actually retrievable.

United’s basic problem out of possession was the hybrid. They pressed half man-oriented, but with a plus-one in the last line — a construction that forces compromises in both directions. It demands access up front that it doesn’t fund at the back.

There were certainly moments when it worked. When Dalot pushed through into the press on the right and passed his man backwards, a clean man-orientation emerged all the way up to the front — and with it a clear assignment for Mount, out of which the ball could be won. But that stayed the exception. Most of the time Dalot sat too flat out of possession, so United couldn’t trigger the duels up front and went into the press a man short.

Mount was the most conspicuous case in this respect. He barely got into a duel across the whole match because the decision to step out always came too late. Against a team that works with preparatory movements, a late decision is practically the same as none: the opponent already has the head start before the journey even begins.

On top of that came a problem on the far side. There, United’s winger frequently failed to protect the depth — usually Mbeumo, who jogged back rather than closing the space. But that was precisely the target zone of Como’s diagonal switches. It allowed them to change sides almost at will and regularly find an open structure on the other side.

The second half brought no improvement. The distances to opponents stayed large, and centrally what was actually intended still didn’t materialise. Michael Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring more physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it didn’t solve the problem — the assignments still didn’t work, the duels in the centre still didn’t happen, and the wide positions dragged the eights out further, which opened new spaces.

In the end the energy simply wasn’t there to press with the necessary intensity. United’s press became increasingly static, and a static press against an opponent with this passing quality yields not a single ball win — it only hands them the starting positions they’re looking for anyway.

Como started the game in a high press and sustained it above all in the first half, with isolated spells after the break too. It was triggered on back-passes and goal kicks — and, remarkably, by their own goals: after every strike the team immediately pushed high again instead of managing the lead in a deeper block.

The steering ran consistently towards United’s left build-up side. The reason is personnel: Harry Maguire played there with his weaker left foot as the outside foot, and next to him stood another right-footer in Noussair Mazraoui on the left. Two right-footers on one build-up side means the ball always has to be taken back inside to play on — exactly where Como were closing the door. The press was usually triggered by the left-sided forward, with the ball ending up at either Maguire or Mazraoui.

Pressing structure after the change in the second half.

In the centre Como defended man-oriented, with both sixes pushing very high and taking flat passes into United’s midfielders out of the game. That removed the short route through the middle as an option and left the long ball. But the long ball triggered nothing for United: the second balls went to Como practically every time.

That isn’t chance, it’s running work. The sixes consistently tracked the negative pressing paths back, anticipated the long ball early, and were in front of the line before it arrived. Luis Milla covered the most ground of any player on the pitch in this pattern — recovery runs after being played past, doubling up on the ball, attacking the second ball. When the long ball came from Maguire or one of the full-backs, a six was regularly in front of the line with an intense sprint, sealing the centre, taking away the diagonal follow-up passes and attacking the loose balls.

Defending the long ball and extra support on the second ball.

Once the ball was on one side, that side was isolated. The near-side forward had access to the centre-back and dropped back a touch when the pass went to the full-back, so that he covered centre-back and six simultaneously and closed the square pass. The far-side forward marked the goalkeeper. That served two functions at once: it took away United’s escape route via the keeper along with the switch, and it kept a player in position for the moment of the turnover.

The double pivot worked in a deliberate half-position. The near-side six stayed tight to his man, the far-side one dropped slightly behind him — securing the space and at the same time establishing access to the far-side centre-back in United’s back three.

The last line defended largely man-for-man in the high press, and very tightly. But the decisive detail is the one that carries the whole concept: the man-marking near the ball applied only to the action in progress. Around it, players were already releasing from their assignments and orienting towards the second ball. That is only retrievable at very high intensity — and it’s the reason United’s forced long balls produced not a single usable moment.

When Como dropped a line, the man-orientation at the second height stayed exceptionally high. Here too the back-pass served as the trigger to push up as a unit and tip back into man-marking. If United were in a closed body position, the Comaschi had already pushed up far enough to attack the back-pass at the moment it was played — a pattern that led directly to the 3-0. The half-position was at work there again: closing down level with the centre-back while simultaneously prepared to push through onto the next player deeper.

In the first half Como still had problems assigning the half-positions against ManU’s 3-2-5. In this example Fernandes moves to the wing, Como fail to establish responsibility and access and are played through at mid-height.

Up to half-time, though, Como had a structural problem with this. The pure man-marking didn’t come off cleanly, because of the two sixes in front of the line, one should really have tucked in and taken over the half-position for the centre-backs. That didn’t happen consistently. The consequence: a centre-back stepped out, the space behind him stayed unoccupied, and that was exactly what United kept attacking.

The pattern of pressing onto Manchester United’s left side continued in the second half, now with Dalot at full-back. The press was still triggered by Douvikas, with the ball-near winger pushing up as a second striker to press the centre-back. Paz focused on the visitors’ ball-near six and Couto pushed through onto Dalot. Through the central connections the game was isolated on one side.

At half-time Como adjusted. One six moved directly into the line, with the far-side winger pushing in onto the six — they now pressed out of a 5-2-1-2. Lucas Da Cunha pressed through from the right onto Maguire, while Nico Paz picked up United’s six. The central forward pressed the central centre-back and from there carried on to the goalkeeper. The far-side wide midfielder and the remaining six were poised — one onto United’s free six, one onto the far-side centre-back — again in that half-position between opponent and the space to be protected.

ManU building up high against the man-oriented Italians.

The effect was immediately visible. Como controlled the half-positions, got access right into the last line throughout, and set the rhythm of the match from then on. The adjustment solved exactly the problem the first half had left open, without changing the underlying principle.

In the final phase Como defended from a very deep line inside the box. That left barely any space behind the line to play into — which United hardly looked for anyway. The visitors attacked little depth and rarely occupied the width in the final third, so Como could stay compact without being punished for it.

Accordingly, a lot of players stood very centrally in and in front of the box. That produced numerous cover shadows and a numerical superiority around the ball, which allowed them to steer actively within the block rather than merely react. At times they deliberately steered inside, to guide the opponent into the prepared congestion and cover from a deeper position.

Deep back line in the box, centre closed and play steered inside. The box defending allowed the English side shots from distance.

The crucial point is that Como left the gaps open not despite this set-up but because of it. If you pack the box and secure the depth, you give up the space in front of the line — and that is exactly where United were supposed to shoot from.

The numbers vindicate the decision. Taking the conceded goal out of the box, Como allowed nine shots from outside the penalty area across ninety minutes, worth roughly 0.24 xG combined; only three of them were even on target. On top of that, of the four shots inside the box, two came from at least 14 to 15 metres and effectively belong in the long-range category as well. United accepted the invitation, and the shooting quality was perfectly decent — it’s just that with that scoring probability over a whole match, the most likely outcome is precisely what happened.

1-0, Nico Paz (2′) — The goal comes before United are even set. Como take the goal kick quickly and play straight to the full-back, luring the visitors into the press — only not cleanly. Mason Mount triggers the pressure and is played past, and on the second ball he goes into the duel as well instead of holding the middle and looking for the space where he could pick the ball up. That is exactly what opens the centre, the space for the second ball is there, and Paz can play it through.

What follows is the transition principle in its purest form: a quick switch to the diagonal side, then short passes against a very deep line. Matheus Cunha doesn’t pick up the player behind him and leaves the ten space open, where Paz is standing. He needs two touches because neither Cunha from above nor Maguire from his distance gets into the duel. Lammens, for his part, doesn’t defend the goal with his cover shadow but takes the near post — which leaves the far corner to a strong left foot. The curled finish into it is quality, though.

2-0, Anastasios Douvikas (49′, assist Paz) — Same zone, same cause. Bryan Mbeumo doesn’t choose to shut the centre but closes the centre-back, which creates the space for Da Cunha, who drives forward and plays Martin Baturina in. Centrally, Mount again fails to pick up his man. Out wide, Amad Diallo, introduced at half-time, generates no pressure centrally, so Mazraoui can’t take over the deep wide player in the first place.

The result is Paz behind the opponent, in the ten space, dribbling unopposed at goal. Matthijs de Ligt establishes no reference to Douvikas’s run in behind and slips as well — a one-against-one with the goalkeeper that Douvikas finishes coolly.

3-0, Nico Paz (57′) — The only goal that comes directly out of the work against the ball. Como are in the midfield press in a closed position with access to the inside lane, win the ball there and punish a lapse of concentration in the visitors’ build-up. At the end, Paz is alone in front of Lammens again.

4-0, Jacobo Ramón (76′, assist Kaiki) — This goal comes from a longer possession spell and shows the assignment problem in its clearest form. Mazraoui is too narrow and has no access to the wide player. Diallo stands in the space with access to neither the central player nor the full-back nor the winger — an orientation that doesn’t work in any direction.

Como switch to Máximo Perrone, who can slide the ball out wide because the space is simply free. From there comes the cross from an open body position, and in the centre Como run a two-against-one at the end of which Ramón scores.

4-1, Joshua Zirkzee (90′) — Como’s only lapse, and one in a phase in which the team had already dropped very deep. United get the ball to the wing, it’s two against two in the box, and the cross is initially only feinted. Zirkzee orients himself well forwards at that moment, while Como’s defenders are already stepping out after the feinted cross. Diallo’s ball in behind the line is strong, the offside call is razor-thin in his favour, and Zirkzee takes two touches before finishing one-against-one with the goalkeeper. A blemish, not a structural problem.

The common denominator — Three of Como’s four goals come in the same zone: the space in front of and behind United’s line that nobody takes responsibility for. Mount is involved in two of those scenes through an assignment he never establishes, Diallo in two more after coming on. Nico Paz is at the heart of three of the four goals, twice as scorer, once as the provider — always out of the opponent’s blind side.

Como’s 4-1 has one common cause across every phase, and it lies less in any single idea than in its execution under continuous load. Out of possession they steered, isolated and speculated on the second ball — with man-marking that applied near the ball only for the action in progress and was already dissolved around it. In possession they tied opponents in, switched play and worked systematically into the space behind. Both require eleven players to anticipate a teammate’s decision, and both functioned over the full distance.

The connecting detail is the half-position. In the press, the far-side six stood between opponent and the space to be protected; in possession, the eights worked out of the same zone, and the full-backs occupied it flat in order to vacate the wing for exactly those players. Como, in other words, occupied the position in both directions from which two jobs can be done at once — which is why the side could neither be played past in the press nor be pinned in the build-up.

For United the diagnosis is more uncomfortable than the result. The problem wasn’t individual errors but that the team never, in any phase, got into the duels it needed. The mixture of half man-orientation and a plus-one in the last line funded no access up front, and without access every decision is made too late — from Mount in the centre to Mbeumo failing to secure the far-side depth. Carrick’s double change at half-time was meant to bring physicality to the six with Carlos Baleba, but it changed nothing about the assignments, and in Diallo a player came on wide who reached neither one opponent nor the other in his space. That the energy ran out in the end was a consequence rather than a cause.

That leaves the context. A 4-1 against Manchester United is no proof in itself, and Como’s finishing was exceptionally efficient on the night. What showed regardless of the result, though, is a team with a recognisable, rehearsed answer in all four phases of the game — and one that put a technical maturity on the pitch in possession that you wouldn’t readily credit to a side promoted two years ago.

#2486. INTERVIEW: Ex-Dortmund star Patrick Owomoyela on why Jürgen Klopp is "a good fit" for Germany

MÜNCHEN (Bulinews): Ex-Borussia Dortmund star Patrick Owomoyela is confident Jürgen Klopp is the right man who can restore the German national team to it's former glory.

“He is able to change the important things in a short matter of time, and that’s what makes him a good fit," Owomoyela said at a media round in Munich attended by Bulinews.com.

“I wish he could have the Germany guys every day [to practice] but that’s not the case. As I said, he’s able to create an instant impact and a clear vision of what to do.

“Now more than before, his reputation helps players believe what he is saying. All of that is a good fit for a coach with less time but can make things happen.

“It’s just about finding the right people. He used a lot of players [in his first international period] and that will be the case going forward too," the Hamburg native added.

Having played under Klopp at Dortmund for five seasons between 2008 and 2013, Owomoyela gave some fascinating insights into what the 59-year-old demands from his squad.

“He was a coach who pushed for hard work but have fun while doing it. He always had fun it seemed, Even though he asked a lot of us, he would let us have fun as well.

“He was the loudest in the dressing rooms, so he gave you that kind of freedom. But, you had to back it up on the pitch working hard and doing what he asked.

“He could create pictures. He made it really easy to understands where he wanted you to go and what he wanted you to do. It was like a movie in your head.

“So, that kind of ability to get you motivated to do what you need to do, it’s what puts him above many others. He wanted us to play the most intense football.

"It was hunt, hunt, hunt, always hunt. If you got the ball, run fast at your opponent as hard as possible. If they [the opponent] got the ball, go hunt it [back] again.

"He would play games too, if we ran 120 kilometres [as a team] one week, next week he would say let’s do 124 [kilometres]. We believed in what he was saying.

“Even though we lost games and derbies, he would say it doesn’t matter because he felt we were going in the right direction,” he said, speaking with a undeniable passion.

Perhaps the proof is in the pudding. During his time at a once lowly BVB side, he transformed them into a deadly force which claimed two Bundesliga titles and a DFB-Pokal.

Whether that can be replicated on the international stage is up for debate. However, in Owomoyela's mind, Klopp possesses the necessary traits to bring dreams into reality.

#2479. Lazio-Cagliari, Noslin a 2,55: quote gol e precedenti all’Olimpico

Secondo quanto riportato da Agipronews, I biancocelesti, primi in classifica, cercano di conservare la vetta all’Olimpico dopo aver vinto 13 degli ultimi 14 confronti interni con i rossoblù.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

La Lazio non perde da 22 scontri diretti con il Cagliari. L’ultimo successo dei sardi nella Capitale contro i biancocelesti risale al 2009. La squadra di Pisacane arriva alla partita dopo tre sconfitte consecutive: la più recente è maturata a Roma contro i giallorossi di Gasperini.

Per gli uomini di Gattuso, i precedenti casalinghi accompagnano dunque la corsa al primo posto. Il dato delle 13 vittorie nelle ultime 14 gare interne contro il Cagliari è uno degli elementi che precedono la sfida dell’Olimpico.

Nell’ultima partita disputata, sia Lazio sia Cagliari hanno ottenuto almeno un rigore a favore. L’ipotesi di un altro tiro dal dischetto nella gara di domenica è quotata 2,95.

#2477. Hadj Moussa sinks Como as Feyenoord claim first CL group win

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Feyenoord claimed the first win for the Eredivisie teams in the Champions League as they got the better of Italian upstarts Como 1907 in a five-goal thriller.

After a cagey opening, Como took the lead when Assane Diao won a physical challenge on the left with Javi Lopez and drilled the ball past Tjark Ernst from a tight angle.

Feyenoord were level within two minutes as Gjivai Zechiël span away from his marker on the edge of the penalty area and lofted a shot into the top corner.

The visitors suffered a setback when Diao limped off a few minutes later, while Feyenoord grew in confidence, with Givairo Read and Lopez drawing fingertip saves from Como goalkeeper Robert Sanchez just before half-time.

Feyenoord went ahead with the first real chance after the interval, as Anis Haji Moussa cut in from the right and found the bottom corner of Sanchez’ net.

Ten minutes from the end the Algerian appeared to have put the result beyond doubt when his long-range shot came back off the post and deflected in off the goalkeeper’s back to make the score 3-1.

Tasos Douvikas’s injury-time strike for Como ensured a tense last few minutes, but Feyenoord were not to be denied.

Zechiël said the quick reply after Diao put the visitors ahead was crucial for his teammates’ morale. “That gave the team and the stadium the feeling we were going to win today.”

He was full of praise for Hadj Moussa, who had a decisive role in both second-half goals. “Anis is a top-class player. We’re really pleased that he’s still available for us this season.”

The result lifts Feyenoord to 23rd place in the 36-team table, while their next match is a trip to Real Betis next Wednesday, October 21.

#2475. Milan-Inter 3-1: Moreira firma un gol e due assist nel derby

Amorim vince la sua prima stracittadina in Serie A; per la squadra di Chivu è l’ottavo derby consecutivo senza successi.

Il Milan batte l’Inter 3-1 nel derby di Halloween. Secondo quanto riportato da SportMediaset, Pulisic e Rabiot segnano nei primi dieci minuti, Bastoni accorcia e Moreira firma la terza rete rossonera prima dell’intervallo. La squadra di Ruben Amorim sale a 21 punti, quattro in meno dell’Inter, che potrebbe essere staccata dalla Lazio nella gara in programma il giorno seguente.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Moreira è il protagonista della vittoria del Milan, efficace nelle ripartenze contro un’Inter in difficoltà all’avvio e meno incisiva dopo il riposo. Cristian Chivu schiera i nerazzurri con il 3-4-2-1: Thuram è l’unica punta, sostenuto da Sucic e Diouf. Il francese resta però isolato, mentre i rossoneri prendono subito l’iniziativa.

Al 4’ Moreira supera Carlos Augusto e mette il pallone in area. Pulisic lo colpisce di testa e porta avanti il Milan. Per l’Inter è l’ottava partita stagionale cominciata con uno svantaggio. Al 10’ arriva anche il raddoppio: sugli sviluppi di un corner, Rabiot deposita in rete approfittando degli errori della difesa e di Josep Martinez, schierato al posto dell’influenzato Provedel.

I nerazzurri cercano una risposta sui calci piazzati. Carlos Augusto si rende pericoloso e al 22’ l’Inter accorcia: Akanji fa da sponda su calcio d’angolo, Bastoni conclude in gol. La squadra di Chivu prende poi il controllo della partita e spinge alla ricerca del pareggio. Maignan respinge il tentativo di Calhanoglu e interviene anche su Spence.

Al 43’ il Milan colpisce ancora. Moreira calcia una punizione che attraversa la barriera apertasi davanti a lui; Martinez parte in ritardo e viene sorpreso dalla traiettoria. Il primo tempo si chiude così sul 3-1.

Chivu riparte senza sostituzioni e conferma il 3-4-2-1. Thuram rimane isolato, mentre l’Inter perde la spinta mostrata prima dell’intervallo. Carlos Augusto prova a rendersi pericoloso con un colpo di testa in tuffo. I cambi che portano Dimarco e le soluzioni offensive con Pio Esposito e Bonny ai lati di Thuram arrivano soltanto nel finale: restano dieci minuti, più il recupero. Il Milan si protegge senza correre rischi rilevanti e costruisce un’occasione con Cissé. Per Amorim è una vittoria nella sua prima stracittadina di Serie A.

Milan (3-4-2-1): Maignan; Gila, De Winter, Pavlovic; Moreira (44’ st Terracciano), Musah (37’ st Jashari), Modric, Diawara (37’ st Bartesaghi); Rabiot, Pulisic (37’ st Cissé); Gonçalo Ramos (44’ st Hutchinson). A disposizione: Bouyer, Torriani, Estupiñán, Comotto, Vladimirov, Ossola, Perera, Menon, Vos, Camarda. Allenatore: Ruben Amorim.

Inter (3-4-2-1): J. Martinez; Bisseck (36’ st Bonny), Akanji, Bastoni (36’ st Esposito); Spence, Barella (36’ st Dimarco), Calhanoglu (27’ st Zielinski), Carlos Augusto; Diouf (27’ st Mkhitaryan), Sucic; Thuram. A disposizione: Di Gennaro, Taho, Stankovic, Bovio, Mosconi, Jakirovic, Lavelli. Allenatore: Cristian Chivu.

Marcatori: 4’ Pulisic (M), 10’ Rabiot (M), 22’ Bastoni (I), 43’ Moreira (M). Ammoniti: De Winter (M), Moreira (M), Carlos Augusto (I).

#2474. Bayern frustrated, Stuttgart bounce back and Elversberg run riot – 5 things we learned from Bundesliga Matchday 8

Eight matches into the Bundesliga season, Bayern Munich and Borussia Dortmund remain unbeaten at the top of the league, locked on 18 points after their showdown at the Allianz Arena.

Elsewhere, Freiburg gained ground on the top two, Werder Bremen's run continued, and Stuttgart finally showed signs of life on their trip to Leverkusen.

Looking back at the weekend, here are the five biggest talking points from Matchday 8.

On an evening when Harry Kane was honoured for winning the Ballon d’Or, the Englishman endured a rare frustrating night as Bayern Munich were held to a draw by Borussia Dortmund.

Kane struck the crossbar from the penalty spot, as Bayern hit the woodwork four times and twice let their lead slip away.

Dortmund, however, deserve credit for their persistence.

After struggling for attacking creativity in recent weeks, Niko Kovac’s side showed far more ambition in Munich, with goals from Serhou Guirassy and Fabio Silva sealing a point on their travels.

However, Bayern will feel they should have claimed all three points, with the side coached by Aaron Danks producing an xG of 4.48 compared to Dortmund's 0.74 of Dortmund, while Lennart Karl came close to netting a winner in the dying moments as his effort from distance struck the bar.

Then, there may also be bigger concerns in the treatment room, with Ismael Saibari, Serge Gnabry, and Michael Olise all forced off through injury ahead of Tuesday's trip to Atletico Madrid - a more than difficult place to go with a thin squad.

With boss Vincent Kompany still recovering and Bayern looking increasingly light across the squad, Tuesday's clash could be a real banana skin, but it could also be a real statement as the international break nears.

A week ago, Stuttgart were sitting 15th after suffering an embarrassing home defeat to a Gladbach side that had previously lost every Bundesliga match.

Saturday’s emphatic victory at Bayer Leverkusen was quite the response.

Ermedin Demirovic struck twice, with Jamie Leweling and Bilal El Khannouss also on target as Sebastian Hoeneß’s side produced comfortably their best attacking performance of the campaign.

It was a result that VfB had been threatening since the start of the campaign, having created bucketloads of chances in recent defeats, only to end up lacking the edge in the final third.

However, Hoeneß's side's xG sat at 3.23 on Saturday afternoon, and for once they overperformed rather than underperformed, while skipper Deniz Undav also played an important part, creating Demirovic's second goal, and Dennis Seimen impressed on his return between the posts.

Having lost five of their opening seven matches, Stuttgart desperately needed a result of this magnitude.

They remain in the bottom half, but Saturday offered a glimpse of the side that supporters have become accustomed to watching under Hoeneß.

The challenge now is producing that on a consistent basis, with the midweek clash with Galatasaray to come next.

It is becoming increasingly difficult to describe Elversberg’s results as surprises.

The Bundesliga newcomers recorded their fourth victory in eight matches on Friday evening, putting four past Mainz in another thoroughly entertaining performance.

Felix Keidel’s excellent free-kick set Vincent Wagner’s side on their way, before Francis Onyeka and Maurice Krattenmacher established a three-goal advantage before half-time.

Cole Campbell then provided the highlight of the evening with a spectacular acrobatic finish in stoppage time.

With 13 points already collected, the Saarland club are comfortably holding their own in their first Bundesliga season.

Mainz, meanwhile, have now suffered three consecutive defeats in all competitions and were particularly poor during the opening 45 minutes.

Nobody at Elversberg will be getting carried away after eight matches, but their early results suggest they have every reason to believe they belong at this level.

After consecutive league defeats to Leverkusen and Bayern, Freiburg looked set for another disappointing afternoon when they trailed Augsburg at half-time on Saturday.

However, Julian Schuster’s substitutions completely changed the contest.

Igor Matanovic levelled the score after being introduced at the break, before fellow substitute Cyriaque Irie completed the comeback five minutes from time.

Remarkably, all four players directly involved in Freiburg’s two goals had started the afternoon on the bench, with Keisuke Goto and Vincenzo Grifo providing the assists.

“We told the lads last night that there was a good chance their moment would come today,” Schuster explained afterwards.

Freiburg were far from their best, with Augsburg enjoying several promising opportunities, but the visitors took theirs when it mattered.

The victory leaves Freiburg third on 16 points, just two behind Bayern and Dortmund.

The goals are not flowing as they were at the start of the season, but the Black Forest club are more than capable of grinding out wins when called upon, as seen in the cup against Wolfsburg last week, and against Augsburg too.

With a clash against Union Berlin to come next weekend, there is every chance that Freiburg go into the international break sitting in the Champions League spots - fully deserved after an excellent start to the term.

Niclas Füllkrug’s goalscoring exploits have dominated the conversation around Werder Bremen in recent weeks, and understandably so.

The 33-year-old continued his remarkable run against Hoffenheim on Saturday, scoring for the seventh consecutive Bundesliga match to take his season tally to nine.

However, Daniel Thioune will have been equally pleased with what happened at the other end.

Werder recorded a clean sheet against a Hoffenheim side that had scored three against Bayer Leverkusen the previous weekend, with Karl Hein impressing between the sticks, before Eren Dinkci sealed the victory with a superb finish late on.

As a result, Bremen's unbeaten Bundesliga run now sits at seven matches, leaving Die Grün-Weißen sitting fifth in the standings just half a year on from battling to stay in the division.

There was some concern surrounding Mitchell Weiser, who departed with an apparent muscular injury just weeks after returning from a lengthy absence, but with Füllkrug scoring regularly and the backline improving, Thioune has plenty to be pleased about.

Bremen have put 15 points on the board in eight outings, and like Freiburg, could well find themselves in the top four over the international break if they seal a strong result in Stuttgart next week.

#2473. Gonini su Floriani Mussolini e la Lazio: «Veniva attaccato, ma era e resta un ragazzo d’oro»

L’ex allenatore alla Vigor Perconti racconta gli sguardi rivolti al ragazzo e ciò che percepiva nei suoi silenzi.

Piero Gonini, che ha allenato Romano Floriani Mussolini alla Vigor Perconti, ha ricordato in un’intervista il periodo trascorso con l’attuale terzino della Lazio. Ha parlato del suo arrivo a 15 anni, delle prove in campo e delle qualità mostrate nel ruolo di terzino. Ha raccontato anche come, a suo giudizio, il ragazzo vivesse il peso del proprio cognome e gli attacchi ricevuti.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Nel ricordo di Gonini, Floriani Mussolini era un quindicenne dalla corporatura esile. Pur giocando nella Lazio, si presentò alla Vigor Perconti senza un atteggiamento di superiorità verso gli altri. L’allenatore ne descrive così l’aspetto e il modo di porsi: «Romano era magro, fino fino, ma non arrivò con la spocchia di chi giocava nella Lazio, bensì con umiltà».

Per Gonini, quell’umiltà è rimasta una caratteristica del ragazzo conosciuto durante l’esperienza alla Vigor Perconti. Nel rievocare quel periodo, il tecnico affianca il ricordo del suo comportamento a quello delle prove svolte per trovare la posizione in cui potesse esprimersi meglio.

Gonini racconta che Floriani Mussolini fu impiegato anche a centrocampo, con il numero 8. Il suo giudizio sul ruolo in cui rendeva di più, però, riguarda la fascia difensiva: «Aveva 15 anni, lo provammo anche a centrocampo, col numero 8, ma come terzino faceva la differenza».

Tra le qualità che l’ex allenatore associa a quel ruolo ci sono la corsa e la continuità con cui il giocatore si proponeva in sovrapposizione. «Ricordo la sua bella corsa e la sua capacità di sovrapporsi di continuo», dice Gonini. Accanto alla valutazione delle sue prestazioni in campo, il tecnico esprime anche un giudizio personale su Romano, definendolo «Un ragazzo d’oro».

#2472. No Picnic: inquiry reveals Dutch cycling team’s toxic culture

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

The Dutch cycling team Picnic-PostNL has been silently struggling with an oppressive atmosphere and culture of intimidation, according to an investigation published by broadcaster NOS and cycling platform In De Waaier.

Many of the team’s former members have been left traumatised and described a “culture of fear” and “unsafe working conditions”.

Reporters confidentially spoke with 71 current and former employees of the team, which competes in the World Tour, the sport’s top division. It currently employs around 120 people. Many people described how management would punish criticism and ignore mental health complaints.

One interviewee described the management structure as a “dictatorship” and another said team members were treated as “puppets”.

This began after the team’s successes in the Tour de France in 2013 and 2014, when German rider Marcel Kittel won stages as a sprinter. After that, the managers became paranoid about losing control of the team, one person told NOS.

More rules were established and people were told “everyone is replaceable.”

After Dutch racer Tom Dumoulin won the 2017 Giro d’Italia, one of cycling’s three Grand Tours, the managers became even more “radicalised”, according to another interviewee.

Dutch rider Martijn Tusveld described how he was dropped from major races for questioning the team’s tactics. He became so anxious he considered harming himself. He left the team and said he is still mentally unwell.

Another, who was suffering mentally, said he was told by sports director Rudi Kemna “I don’t believe in psychologists” after he asked for help.

The Dutch professional cyclists’ association VVBW told NOS it had heard similar accounts over the past 18 months, with some riders calling in tears.

NOS has asked the team owner Iwan Spekenbrink for a comment but he has so far refused to reply to any allegations in person or in writing.

Forest fires occur every summer in Portugal but the tragedy that struck the community of Pedrógão Grande in the centre of the country is unprecedented. Over 60 people lost their lives, many trapped in their cars when trying to flee from the flames.

This Just Giving page is a simple way for people, wherever they are in the world, to make a contribution to help rebuild these communities and bring some comfort to those who lost everything in the blink of an eye. The money raised will be sent to a network of local authorities and charities working in the area affected. Thank you for your support.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Gli highlights della partita saranno disponibili circa un’ora dopo il fischio finale.

Contro i rossoblù, la Lazio segna in casa da 14 partite consecutive, per un totale di 31 reti. L’ultima gara all’Olimpico senza gol biancocelesti risale al 28 ottobre 2009: finì 0-1 e resta anche l’ultimo successo del Cagliari a Roma. I dati sono riportati da Lega Serie A.

I biancocelesti hanno inoltre la percentuale di conversione realizzativa più alta, il 16,8%, e hanno mandato in gol 12 giocatori diversi, più di ogni altra squadra. Il Cagliari è fermo a quattro marcatori differenti, come il Venezia; solo il Parma ne conta meno, con tre.

Gli avversari dei sardi effettuano in media 18,4 tiri a partita: soltanto Atalanta, con 18,9, e Frosinone, con 18,8, ne concedono di più. Il possesso palla del Cagliari è del 41,9%; le uniche percentuali inferiori sono quelle di Lecce, al 41%, e Parma, al 41,8%.

#2464. Top five football betting tips and tricks

Football is the world’s most famous sport, and it is the sport of choice of a great many sports punters as well. Especially popular in Europe, betting on football can be an incredibly profitable enterprise, so long as you know how to do it.

Getting started on football betting is far from easy, though. This form of gambling requires dedication, investment, and persistence. As you gain experience, your intuition will improve and you will be able to make more correct predictions.

But experience is not the only thing that can help you become a better football punter. In fact, you can substantially improve your betting strategy by simply following some basic football betting tips.

You might find shopping around boring and unhelpful, but you just have to do it when you search sites for football betting. As you know, the higher the odds, the bigger the payout. What you may have missed, however, is that odds are different at each bookmaker. Having said that, shopping around for the best odds is sensible, as it rewards you with bigger payouts.

In this line of thought, sticking to one bookmaker is virtually pointless. Even if the bookie of your choosing offers the best odds for a number of matches, it is highly unlikely that their odds will remain the best for long.

An obvious one, we know. And yet, you will be surprised by the number of football bettors who wager on football, but do not follow the sport. So, why is it important to follow the action closely?

First of all, by watching football matches, you can take part in the most exciting football betting market – the live betting market. What is more important, however, is that following the action provides an invaluable insight into teams and players’ style, condition, and determination – important factors  to consider when placing a bet.

As you watch more and more games, you will improve your analytic abilities. In time, you will be able to make more accurate predictions about the outcome of different matches, which, of course,  will result in placing more winning bets.

As you know, odds are based on the probability of occurrence of a certain outcome; the higher the odds, the more unlikely it is for that outcome to actually occur. But what happens when the odds offered by the bookmaker represent a probability that is lower than the actual probability of an outcome occurring? In such a situation, you can place what is called a value bet.

If you want to become a professional football punter, you should always search for value when betting, as it leads to huge profits

Favoured by beginners in particular, accumulators are a type of football wager which allows you to bet on as many fixtures as you like. Most commonly, you will be asked to choose a match winner, but as the market is diverse, you may be offered a number of other betting options. What makes accumulators an appealing betting market is the huge potential payout – here, you can stake as little as £1 and have the potential to win more than £500,000, depending on the bookmaker and the number of selected fixtures.

If you find accumulators’ payouts too good to be true, you are right. As a matter of fact, there is a reason for the odds to be set so high – actually winning an accumulator bet, especially one which involves a wide selection of matches, is nearly impossible. You see, all you need for your whole wager to lose is one wrong prediction. For example, if you bet on 10 matches and just one of them goes wrong, your entire accumulator is lost.

To make money by betting on football, avoid betting on accumulators – the risk is simply not worth it.

Finally, if you want to be a successful football punter, learn how not to mix emotions with betting. This is easier said than done, though.

Whenever your favourite team plays, you want them to win, regardless of how (im)possible that may be. If you do not learn how to leave emotions out of betting, however, you are highly likely to put money on your favourite team even if they have no chance of winning the match. Conversely, should you base your bets on hard facts and not on emotions, you increase your chances to win.

Our advice – avoid betting on fixtures that involve one of your favourite teams, that is the easiest way to keep emotions at bay.

#2462. England’s Dutch coach Sarina Wiegman extends contract until 2029

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Dutch football coach Sarina Wiegman has extended her contract with the England women’s team by two years, taking her up to the 2029 European Championships.

Wiegman, 56, led the team to two European titles in 2022 and 2025, as well as their first World Cup final in 2023, when they lost to Spain.

Prior to that she won the European Championships with the Dutch national team in 2017 and reached the World Cup final two years later, losing 2-0 to the United States.

“I am so happy to be able to carry on with this squad and cannot think of a better job in football,” she said in a statement released by the English Football Association (FA).

“Working for a world-class organisation like the FA, and alongside so many people dedicated to winning England teams and the growth of our game, excites me every day.”

FA chief executive Mark Bullingham described Wiegman as “an incredible coach with an unparalleled record in major tournaments”. He added: “There is no one better to lead the Lionesses into the next two tournaments.”

Commentators had widely expected the Dutchwoman to leave her post after next year’s World Cup, after England failed to qualify automatically for the tournament.

A 4-0 loss to Spain in June, the team’s heaviest defeat since Wiegman took charge, means they will have to come through a play-off tournament with Greece, Ukraine and Slovakia to reach the finals in Brazil.

Wiegman said: “Anyone who knows me will understand that my main concern right now is absolutely the World Cup, and helping this squad realise their potential in the years to come.

“We have an important job to do to confirm our place in Brazil, and hopefully we can do that and give the country another special summer to remember next year.”

#2460. High-flying Portuguese footballers aiming to conquer Europe again

For opposition teams at the upcoming European Championship, the list of Portugal’s best footballers makes formidable reading. Magnificent performances throughout 2020/21 and the conquering of silverware and awards in major leagues during the season suggests the Seleção is in good shape to defend its crown this summer.

Football has always been by far the biggest sport in Portugal. The fact it dominates all others in terms of popularity, players and investment and its huge social and cultural weight in the nation helps explain why this small country has been able to produce many of the best and most famous football players across the world. The Euro 2016 victory further solidified football’s place as a sport with an enormous “feel-good” factor among the country’s general population.

The positive vibes associated with the national team in particular is reflected in the fact that the year’s biggest football event will feature several Portuguese players who are currently among the most talked about and most successful individuals in the game.

Cristiano Ronaldo is the best Portuguese footballer and probably the best player in the world! His full name is Cristiano Ronaldo dos Santos Aveiro and he was born in 1985. His position has evolved from a winger to a forward over his long career, with no let-up in the phenomenal success he has enjoyed. He currently plays for Italian giants Juventus, and despite a disappointing season for the storied club, Ronaldo was as effective as ever, scoring 36 goals and finishing 2020/21 as top scorer in Italy.

#2458. PSV hold on to book narrow Champions League win in Porto

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

PSV survived a second-half onslaught in Porto to secure their first win of the Champions League group stages.

Guus Til scored the only goal when he tapped in from close range after Esmir Bajraktarevic’s shot had come back off the post and was steered across goal by Sven Mijnans.

The Eindhoven club were worth their narrow lead against Francesco Farioli’s side after dominating possession in the first half, but rarely troubled Diogo Costa in the Porto goal.

Til went close with a header early in the second half before putting PSV ahead in the 57th minute.

But the visitors had to defend heroically as Porto poured forward in search of an equaliser, aided by an increasingly fragile Matej Kovar.

The goalkeeper put his defenders in jeopardy with a weak clearance in the 72nd minute, but redeemed himself by saving Victor Froholdt’s shot with his chest before Lutsarel Geertruida blocked the follow-up from Pepê.

A minute later Kovar was left stranded after he rushed out of his penalty area, but Sergiño Dest managed to hack the ball away before Alberto Costa could poke it into the open goal.

At the other end, substitute Denis Man came close to scoring a second for PSV with his first touch when his curling shot beat Costa but failed to sneak inside the post.

The 1-0 win lifts PSV to a provisional 16th place in the Champions League table at the halfway stage of round three, while Porto are still looking for their first point.

Their next match is at home to Belgian side Club Brugge, who will make the short trip to Eindhoven on November 4.

#2457. Galatasaray boss acknowledges summer interest in Stuttgart duo

Bilal El Khannouss was linked with a move away from VfB Stuttgart over the summer, but ultimately stayed with the Bundesliga club.

Speaking to Kicker, Galatasaray director of development Fatih Demireli has now confirmed that the Turkish giants explored a move for the attacking midfielder.

“We had good discussions with him, with VfB Stuttgart and with those around him, but at some point decisions have to be made when it comes to transfers. In the end, we signed Aleksey Batrakov and are very pleased with his development. He has enormous potential, and we want to support him in the best possible way as he continues his journey," he said.

El Khannouss was not the only VfB Stuttgart player to attract interest from Galatasaray, as Ermedin Demirovic was also on their radar. Like his teammate, however, the striker remained at the Bundesliga club.

“Different opportunities arise over the course of a transfer window. In the end, we consciously opted for other solutions,” Demireli explained, admitting:

“It's true that we were interested. Many factors come into play in discussions like these. We also had to take into account the squad regulations in Turkey and ultimately made decisions that we, as a club, are convinced by.”

Stuttgart travel to Turkey to face Galatasaray in the Champions League on Tuesday.

#2456. Opinion: The morality of football

Yesterday evening, Selecao correspondent Nathan Motz accepted a unique opportunity to appear as a guest on the Moral Maze – a BBC radio broadcast dealing with matters of moral and ethical significance.

Is football a moral force for good? That was the question of the day, and each of the four guests were asked to discuss various aspects of the debate from financial fairplay to club takeovers by foreign entities to racism.

Nathan’s segment was concerned with whether football has transcendent moral value, and what that is exactly. Is there a greater significance to the game we are watching, or is it merely 22 humans kicking a leather ball into a net?

Join Nathan as he elaborates on the most significant takeaways of what was a fascinating discussion on the sport and morality in general.

Football, for some, may be the most poignant reminder of the moral universe in which we live. While debate has raged for centuries regarding the precise origin of moral absolutes and how humans ought to live, it is simply unavoidable to say humanity is often troubled by events surrounding the game we all love so dearly.

In not so distant history, every normal act of human life would have been assigned some degree of moral significance. Every action had inherently moral indicators - for good or for evil. Today, postmodern views of morality dominate. These describe morality as an entirely subjective dimension. In other words, it cannot be pinned down exactly whether behaviors are objectively good or bad. We can only find fault with someone else’s interpretation based on their own unique moral code. Morality is relative to the individual or society.

Yet last night’s dialogue concerning football and human behavior was filled with moral accusations and claims which strongly suggested humans are not content with relativist morality. Indeed, we are at times frightened by and disgusted with what we observe in and around football. Our sense of moral justice is deeply disturbed. But with respect to what exactly?

In responding to the panelist’s questions, I observed a familiar paradigm. Each time I have discussed any aspect of moral behavior, the speculation humans are good or at worst morally neutral animals continually reemerges. This widely accepted presupposition is then followed shortly by disgust at some aspect of human behavior. Almost as if to ask “why are all these good humans behaving so badly?” while ignoring the very real implications of this line of inquiry. A question rendered unanswerable by the insistent a priori assumptions from which it was derived.

If humans are intrinsically beneficent, conscientious, fair, humble, and honest then why is football rife with racism, misogyny, greed, disenfranchisement, lies, cheating, violence, and the hypocrisy to deny the occurrence of any such behaviors except when a rival club is the guilty party? Behaviors which are perpetrated by individual supporters, fanatical groups, club administrations, corporate bodies such as FIFA, political entities, nations, and even the players themselves. No one participating faction is guiltless.

To so blithely sidestep more sufficient explanations for the empirical reality of wickedness in football and in human behavior more generally is not just factually incorrect. It undermines real attempts to understand the root of these behaviors where they might be dealt with more effectively.

To listen to The Moral Maze episode entitled “Football: a moral force for good?” produced by BBC radio and featuring PortuGOAL’s Seleção correspondent Nathan Motz click on this link

Furthermore, in good faith, how successful is a response against heinous evil when those perpetrating it are regarded as behaving in accord with their unique moral ambitions? Humans refuse to accept their indisputably objective moral lacking at a fundamental level. This restricts us from ever doing any more than “stamping out” the alleged impropriety of others – while we ourselves may readily engage in these very same behaviors. Moral relativism cannot account for the moral outrage emanating from all corners of the football world. Is Qatar wrong to perpetrate human rights atrocities or is it allowable because their culture is indifferent? Is hooliganism objectively wrong, or only when someone else’s club is responsible?

The overlooking of our own failings while lashing out at others for theirs is quintessential to the morally fallen nature of humanity itself. Football tells all. There are not good people and bad people. There are only people who both willfully and unintentionally transgress the moral code embedded in each of our hearts. A moral law which can only have ever been given by a moral Lawgiver. Otherwise, it is truly your word against mine as it pertains to the moral validity of all behaviors - even those which may deeply violate your conscience.

Football generates an episodic, semantic, and imagistic description of universal archetypes: good and evil, sacrifice and greed, cruelty and mercy, hero and anti-hero. It speaks to every arc of life’s plotline: tragedy, comedy, victory, defeat, the finite nature of our circumstances. It is one of the preeminent existential dialects alongside religion, visual art, written and spoken language, music, and theatre. Football plucks a primal chord.

As metaphysics have been curtailed to just physics in the modern secular West, football has only elevated our awareness of the often dire moral predicaments of our species. Yet it is clear the moral conversation does not originate on a football pitch. The pitch is but a grand stage upon which moral themes may be observed and critiqued, a mirror held in front of our own soul.

The desire for ultimate purpose, for meaning beyond the material realm, never disappeared in modernity. Arguably, it has only become more desperate as the outcry for ultimate significance comes under more scrutiny and criticism by those who constrain ontological explanations to those accounted for by physics. The severity of the mental health crisis in the West is in no small part explained by the terrifying existential awareness we are cut adrift in an abstract sea of meaningless, hopeless wandering between two oblivions - that before mortal life began and that at mortal life’s end. Let football be one voice which reassures you - there is ultimate meaning and good reasons why football so unsettles our craving for justice.

In a palpable, unavoidable way, football and supporter communities across Europe are encountering moral quandaries. In Portugal, age-old debates between FC Porto and Benfica have routinely been haunted by, in many cases, farcical and cynical hypocrisy. Throughout its history, the FPF has been susceptible to showing partiality to a club, player, or style which ultimately degrades the performance of our beloved Seleção. Deny it all we like, and not one aspect of this reality is changed.

In conclusion, whatever your thoughts or beliefs on the subject of moral significance, its origin, the meaning of life, and how football draws that out, I hope it will be beneficial to examine these matters more deeply. In my zeal, I admit I have not always been prudent to initiate these discussions in a way which respects thoughtful disagreement. It is my hope this effort to bring that discussion to Portuguese football will be well-received and carefully pondered. But I will contentedly listen to those followers who may be genuinely unhappy or unsupportive of this or any other idea about which I write.

The readers of this website and the larger Portuguese football loving world are my people. It is a joy and a responsibility I take seriously whenever I have the opportunity to raise important questions about the Selecao, the game, and our lives in general.

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Feyenoord endured a frustrating evening in Spain as a penalty by Real Betis’s outstanding player, Isco, secured a 1-0 win for the La Liga side.

The Rotterdammers were second best for most of the game, but held out until the 59th minute, when Abde Ezzalzouli’s cross hit the arm of Jeremiah St. Juste in front of goal.

Isco, who was at the heart of Betis’s attacks all evening, grabbed the ball and gave Tjark Ernst no chance from the penalty spot.

Ernst had made good saves from former AZ Alkmaar striker Troy Parrott and Marc Batra to keep Feyenoord in the game in the first half, but Feyenoord had the best chance of the opening period when Gjivai Zechiël shot over the bar from close range.

The visitors had a claim for a penalty of their own turned down in the 68th minute when Givairo Read claimed he had been pushed by Ezzalzouli, but referee Simone Sozza waved the appeals away.

The result leaves Giovanni van Bronckhorst’s side in 26th place in the Champions League table, with Internazionale up next in De Kuip on November 3.

#2453. Atletico Madrid vs. Bayern Munich preview: Team news and predicted lineups

After being held to a 2-2 draw by Borussia Dortmund in the Bundesliga, an injury-hit Bayern Munich side turn their attention to Tuesday's trip to Spain to face Atletico Madrid in the Champions League.

The Bavarians will be eager to return to winning ways in Europe's premier club competition, having drawn 3-3 against Viking FK and 2-2 with Arsenal in their last two Champions League outings. Those results have left them 14th in the league phase table with five points from three games.

However, Bayern face a tough test against Atletico Madrid, who are particularly strong at the Wanda Metropolitano, where they haven't lost since a 0-1 defeat to Celta Vigo in May last season.

A 1-1 Champions League draw against another Bundesliga side – VfB Stuttgart – was followed by domestic wins over Deportivo La Coruna (1-0) and Levante (2-0), giving Diego Simeone's men a positive run of results heading into Tuesday's clash. Atletico currently sit second in La Liga.

The two sides have met six times over the past 10 years, with Bayern winning three games, Atletico claiming two victories and one ending in a draw. Bayern have never beaten Atletico away from home and will be keen to change that on Tuesday.

Atletico Madrid will have to manage without Morten Hjulmand after he was shown a straight red card against Stuttgart. Johnny Cardoso (thigh) and Koke (muscle) are both doubts.

As for Bayern Munich, assistant coach Aaron Danks will continue deputising for head coach Vincent Kompany, who remains absent after collapsing and being diagnosed with meningeal irritation.

The German champions suffered a triple injury blow at the weekend, as Ismael Saibari, Serge Gnabry and Michael Olise were all forced off during their 2-2 draw with Borussia Dortmund.

Saibari (ankle) and Gnabry (muscle) are both facing longer spells on the sidelines, but Olise could potentially feature after taking part in Bayern's final training session ahead of the game, albeit with his right knee bandaged.

Manuel Neuer also took part in the session and could return from a foot injury, with Jonas Urbig ready to deputise for the veteran goalkeeper once again if he is not passed fit.

Luis Diaz (muscle), Jamal Musiala (thigh) and Bara Sapoko Ndiaye (appendectomy) remain unavailable.

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Feyenoord preserved their four-point advantage over champions PSV after coming away with a point from Eindhoven thanks to an improved second-half performance.

PSV dominated the first half as they deployed a close-passing, tiki-taka style to stifle Feyenoord, but only had Guus Til’s well-taken volley after 20 minutes to show for it.

The Tilburgers remain four points behind ADO Den Haag, who were unable to keep up the momentum of last week’s win against PSV as they lost 3-2 at home to Heerenveen.

Telstar lost by the same margin at Go Ahead Eagles, whose striker Søren Tengstedt netted twice in the first half to lead the Eredivisie scoring charts with eight goals.

#2449. Bayern reveal squad for Atletico Madrid clash

The day before their Champions League clash with Atletico Madrid at the Wanda Metropolitano, Bayern Munich have announced their squad for the game.

Manuel Neuer and Michael Olise have both been included despite recent injury concerns.

Neuer has missed Bayern's last three games due to a foot problem, while Olise was forced off towards the end of Bayern's 2-2 draw with Borussia Dortmund on Saturday after picking up a knock.

However, both players took part in Bayern's final training session ahead of the game, although Olise trained with his right knee bandaged.

Bayern will be without Ismael Saibari and Serge Gnabry, who both picked up more serious injuries against Dortmund.

Luis Diaz, Jamal Musiala and Bara Sapoko Ndiaye are also still unavailable.

#2448. Sports club members becoming less tolerant of homosexuality

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

Acceptance of homosexuality in Dutch sports clubs has declined significantly in the last generation, according to a survey of attitudes by the Mulier Institute.

The organisation found that 66% of people had no objection to a fellow member of a sports club being gay, while in 2008 the figure was 85%.

Just over half (55%) of respondents said they had no problem sharing a shower with a gay team-mate of the same sex, which also represented a drop from 73% in 2008.

The Mulier Institute said the findings reflected a social trend that has also been seen in schools, where the proportion of boys aged between 12 and 16 with a positive view of homosexuality has dropped from 40% to 22% since 2021.

The Dutch Olympic Committee NOC*NSF said the findings were “highly regrettable” and pledged to step up its efforts to make sport more inclusive.

But the John Blankenstein Foundation, which campaigns for LGBT emancipation in sport, said it had noticed declining enthusiasm for its workshops and growing numbers of parents who voiced objections.

Karin Blankenstein, who set up the organisation in 2008, told NOS: “I get the sensitivity around transgender people in sport at the top level, but this is about amateur sport.”

#2446. Bayern Munich : Excellente nouvelle pour Vincent Kompany !

Hospitalisé d'urgence en fin de semaine dernière, Vincent Kompany a pu regagner son domicile ce mercredi 28 octobre 2026. L'entraîneur belge du Bayern Munich va désormais poursuivre sa convalescence au calme, avant d'envisager son retour.

Vincent Kompany avait été hospitalisé d'urgence le vendredi 23 octobre 2026 après avoir été victime d'un malaise au centre d'entraînement de la Säbener Straße, alors qu'il observait une séance de l'équipe réserve du Bayern Munich. Rapidement pris en charge par les services médicaux, le technicien belge a passé plusieurs jours sous observation à l'hôpital. Sur la chaîne allemande ARD, le directeur sportif munichois Max Eberl a d'ailleurs officialisé la cause de cet incident : ce malaise était consécutif à une méningite, soit une inflammation des méninges apparue après un épisode viral. Une alerte sérieuse désormais sous contrôle.

Autorisé à quitter l'hôpital ce mercredi pour poursuivre sa convalescence et son traitement médical à son domicile, Vincent Kompany entame donc une phase de repos indispensable. Selon les informations du journaliste Fabrizio Romano, le Bayern Munich souhaite absolument que son entraîneur se repose et soit à 100 % de sa forme avant d'envisager son retour sur le banc de touche. Aucune prise de risque inutile ne sera tolérée par l'état-major allemand. D'ailleurs, l'entraîneur adjoint Aaron Danks a indiqué qu'aucune date précise de reprise n'était fixée, confirmant que le club bavarois entendait lui laisser tout le temps nécessaire pour récupérer pleinement.

Pendant cette période délicate, le staff bavarois a su parfaitement assurer la continuité sur les terrains. En effet, l'intérim assuré par Aaron Danks s'est soldé par deux victoires probantes en l'espace de quelques jours. Dimanche 25 octobre 2026, les Munichois sont allés s'imposer sur la pelouse du SC Fribourg (3-1) en Bundesliga, avant de signer un large succès face au FC Magdebourg (6-1) le mercredi 28 octobre, à l'occasion du deuxième tour de la Coupe d'Allemagne. Une dynamique sportive rassurante avant un grand rendez-vous national, puisque le Bayern Munich recevra le Borussia Dortmund à l'Allianz Arena le samedi 31 octobre 2026 à 18h30, dans le cadre de la 8e journée de Bundesliga.

#2445. Bayern boss gives fresh injury update on Olise and Neuer ahead of Atletico Madrid trip

Despite Michael Olise and Manuel Neuer both taking part in Bayern Munich's final training session ahead of Tuesday's Champions League away clash against Atletico Madrid, it remains unclear whether – and to what extent – either player will feature.

Olise, who was forced off in the second half of Saturday's 2-2 draw with Borussia Dortmund, trained with his knee bandaged on Monday. Meanwhile, Neuer returned to team training after missing the previous three games due to a foot problem.

Speaking on Monday, Bayern board member for sport Max Eberl provided an update on the duo's fitness.

“As for Michael Olise, we’ll take another look at him tomorrow; we’re taking it day by day," Eberl said.

“It’s good that he’s travelling with us, because he’s an outstanding player who’s always a great asset. However, we’ll avoid the risk of it turning into a long-term injury. Manuel Neuer has been out for quite some time but has been training, so we’ll see how it goes,” he added.

Eberl also stressed Bayern's determination to return to winning ways in the Champions League after drawing their last two games in the competition, while acknowledging how difficult it is to come away with a positive result on the road against Atletico Madrid.

“It’s a top Champions League fixture. We need to show resilience right now. We must keep going if we want to win major titles, and that’s what we want. We want to win in Madrid. The points we dropped in Stavanger are still hanging over us; tomorrow we have a chance to make amends," he said.

“It’s definitely one of the trickiest away fixtures you can face in the Champions League, against opponents who are extremely tough to play against and possess a very high standard of quality, where we’ll have to bring to the game the resilience we’ve had to demonstrate over the past few weeks. They're a team who really will demand everything of us. We’re always under pressure, but we're still heading there to try to achieve the best possible result.”

Xavi Hernandez has his first win as Netherlands head coach as a Mexx Meerdink double led Oranje to a 2-1 win over Serbia in the Nations League.

Xavi made two changes from the side that drew with Germany as Mexx Meerdink replaced the injured Brian Brobbey up front while Joey Veerman came in for Quinten Timber.

Netherlands dominated from the start but they were forced into an early change when Cody Gakpo suffered a knock and Ruben van Bommel came on.

After half an hour, Crysencio Summerville was clipped in the box and Meerdink made it 1-0 from the penalty spot. It was almost 2-0 before the break but Denzel Dumfries could not convert when through on goal.

Serbia made a double change at the break with Luka Jovic and Dusan Tadic coming on. A minute into the second half, the change worked as Jovic was given space in the box to net after Netherlands failed to clear the ball.

Netherlands went in search of the lead again but Tijjani Reijnders blazed a huge chance over while Ruben van Bommel hit the post. Meerdink did eventually make it 2-1 with a lovely finish after a quick 1-2 with substitute Guus Til.

There was to be no hattrick for Meerdink as he was brought off and Netherlands played the final ten minutes with Til through the middle.

Serbia pressed for a late equaliser but the Netherlands had a golden chance to make it 3-1 when Til found himself in on goal. He had Summerville and Van Bommel to his left but overhit the pass and the later’s finish was too weak to find the net.

The Netherlands held on for the victory which takes them to the top of the group with a clash against Greece next on Thursday.

#2443. Big wins for PSV and Twente close gap on leaders Feyenoord

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

A late equaliser for Fortuna Sittard in De Kuip proved costly for Feyenoord as PSV and Twente racked up huge victories to move within two points of the Eredivisie leaders.

Feyenoord paid the price for failing to finish off the Limburgers, with Luciano Valente’s 59th-minute goal the only reward for their dominance.

Fortuna equalised with four minutes left when Shiloh ’t Zand, on loan from Feyenoord, was allowed to run through to the penalty area and beat Tjark Ernst with a low shot.

Feyenoord coach Giovanni van Bronckhorst made no effort to hide his disappointment. “We know that Fortuna are the kind of team who can score from the few attacks they have,” he said. “If you don’t score the goals, things like this can happen.”

Second-placed Ajax are a point behind after a narrow win at home against AZ ended an 11-match winless streak against the Alkmaar club, as Tolu Arokodare’s early header proved decisive.

Twente recorded the biggest win of the weekend with a 9-0 demolition of Excelsior in Rotterdam, with Wout Weghorst scoring four times and Sondre Ørjasaeter notching up five assists.

Excelsior collapsed following the fifth-minute dismissal of Nesto Groen for a reckless high challenge. The home side were 5-0 down at half-time, with Weghorst claiming a hat-trick in the opening 33 minutes.

PSV bagged seven goals in a one-sided encounter with bottom club Willem II, with all the goals coming from different scorers including Ricardo Pepi, who came on in the second half after a six-week absence through injury.

Telstar opened up a three-point gap at the bottom over ADO Den Haag with a 4-2 win against Utrecht in a painful return to Velsen-Zuid for Telstar’s former coach Anthony Correia.

ADO went down 1-0 in Zwolle, with PEC’s Dylan Mbayo scoring the only goal with a first-time finish on the stroke of half-time.

The match between Cambuur Leeuwarden and Go Ahead Eagles was suspended in the 82nd minute after visiting fans threw fireworks onto the pitch.

The clubs agreed with the KNVB not to play the final minutes and allow Cambuur’s 4-0 advantage to stand as the final result.

Go Ahead’s director Jan-Willem van Dop said the club would take action against those responsible: “I am ashamed on behalf a small group of supporters who ruined things for the club and a large hare of the fans in the away end,” he said.

An injury-hit Netherlands side battled back to draw 2-2 against Greece in the Nations League.

Xavi was forced into changes with Cody Gakpo and Jan Paul van Hecke dropping out of the squad. Tijjani Reijnders and Ryan Gravenberch also dropped to the bench with Quinten Timber, Guus Til, Jurrien Timber and Ruben van Bommel starting.

Greece have been in good form recently and their confidence was clear from the start. They had a goal disallowed for offside before Fotis Ioannidis made it 1-0 after 23 minutes.

The Netherlands offered little going forward and in stoppage time, a bad back pass from Van Bommel led to Giorgos Masouras sliding in a second.

The equaliser came in the last minute as Zechiel’s overhead kick dropped into the path of Reijnders who blasted the ball in. Zechiel then came close to a winner but his header was straight at the goalkeeper.

A battling point for the Netherlands, who stay unbeaten under Xavi with a game at home to Serbia to come on Sunday.
