#2325. Spain deliver footballing lesson to send France packing

The great Spanish football revolution transformed the world game in the first decade of the 21st century, and in reaching a second World Cup final, Spain delivered a lesson in Dallas in how to play with deadly effectiveness.

This 2026 France team of stars were, in terms of football ringcraft, handed their passports and boarding cards and waved off at the gate by a Spain team in complete control. The reigning European champions are an unpretentious side, with just the single star in Lamine Yamal, but even he has no choice but to work as part of this cohesive, intelligent team.

Neither Yamal nor any of his team-mates look set to win the 2026 Golden Boot, but on Sunday just outside New York they may take home the only trophy that really matters.

This semi-final was bossed by a midfield triumvirate of Rodri, Dani Olmo and Fabián Ruiz, who did what all the best modern Spanish midfields do to their opposition: spirit away possession, and slowly drain hope. For France, so often the pitch was first too small, and then in an instant much too big. Such is the simple effectiveness of those intense passing triangles that confound the press and preface a switch of play, a new idea, or a ball into a man in space.

The first question from the French media after the game for Didier Deschamps was how he felt about being given such a lesson – and it was hard to argue with that. The marvellous Luis de La Fuente, a man whom France’s 1998 World Cup-winning captain would have breezed past without recognising five years ago, took Deschamps to places where, tactically, he had no answer.

Not for the first time either: in this summer and the previous two, De La Fuente has beaten Deschamps. Two years ago in the semi-final of Euro 2024 and then again in the Nations League semi-final last summer. This was the worst of the three for Deschamps, who will step down after four World Cups in charge of France with more questions about what might have been than that single triumph in 2018.

Mikel Oyarzabal, the 29-year-old Real Sociedad striker, dispatched a penalty won by Yamal in the first half. The Tottenham Hotspur full-back Pedro Porro added the second after half-time with the kind of forward foraging that would have drawn an approving smile from Ange Postecoglou, the man for whom Porro once did that regularly.

Mikel Oyarzabal scores from the spot and Spain lead France! 🇪🇸 pic.twitter.com/pJManVOZf3

There were more Real Madrid players in France shirts than there were playing for Spain, but the club’s new acquisition, Marc Cucurella, was superb again. So too the man formerly known as French, Aymeric Laporte, and others such as Alex Baena and Oyarzabal. None has the profile of France’s biggest names.

To borrow a local analogy, Kylian Mbappé, Ousmane Dembélé and Michael Olise were corralled into the paddock and the gate closed behind them. Deschamps spoke later about his team failing to reach their best levels. He asked rhetorical questions about the referee Iván Barton, from El Salvador, suggesting he had made mistakes. There was a suspicion of handball from Yamal for his penalty award. But in reality the France coach had no definitive answers – he seemed not to know how he had lost this game.

Ultimately, Deschamps placed his faith in the pace and finishing of some of the best attackers in the world, and when they failed to click there was no alternative. Adrien Rabiot was booked early and substituted at half-time. William Saliba became the latest Arsenal player to be injured at this tournament, coming off in the first half.

To counter Mbappé’s pace on a couple of occasions, the Spain goalkeeper Unai Simon took up positions radically far outside his own area to play as a sweeper. Simon was rarely obliged to make a big save and there were even fewer last-ditch challenges from Spanish defenders. Instead, there was an unswerving belief from this Spain team that the game could be controlled and shaped, and France directed away from the areas that made them dangerous.

In the Fifa VIP seats, four greats of Spain’s 2010 World Cup win – Iker Casillas, Sergio Ramos, Xavi Hernandez and Carles Puyol – looked on approvingly. Their side, dominant over two Euros and one World Cup, was different. Built from the great Real Madrid and Barcelona teams of the era, while this Spain side have reached the World Cup final on Sunday without anything like that big-two star power.

“We did it with discipline, organisation, sacrifice,” De La Fuente said. “We did very well at predicting the game’s phases and that’s what we did best as Spanish players. We can interpret games and how to deal with the different phases. It’s the fruit of our work in the academies – and all the coaches. We really need to value what they are doing at that level.”

INCREDIBLE 🔥Spain one-two their way through the France defense and Pedro Porro doubles his country's lead... pic.twitter.com/DFGCLMzFUC

He was right, of course, as the head of a football nation that develops and refines such a high volume of players capable of operating in such harmony. Certainly, they go into Sunday’s final with an extra day’s rest and surely now favourites against either Argentina or England. “You could see this tie as like a final,” De La Fuente said, “and you could say the same for England and Argentina. I don’t prefer one over the other.”

Two years ago in Berlin, De La Fuente’s Spain did much the same to Gareth Southgate’s England in the Euro 2024 final as they did on this occasion. Then, an evening of Spanish control gave way to a gradual realisation that England could not land a punch until it was too late.

The same was the case this time for France. They conceded a penalty after 20 minutes when Lucas Digne swung at a loose ball without checking that Yamal was about to beat him to it. Similar to the penalty Harry Kane conceded against Mexico in the last-16 tie, it was clumsy, but Yamal anticipated the contact. The ball struck his shoulder and was not given as a handball. Later in the second half, Yamal raced past Digne and finished, only to be fractionally offside at the start of his run.

As France struggled to make the connections that are so crucial to them – chiefly Olise and Mbappé – at the other end Spain’s passing was exemplary. There was one lightning sequence, in the 35th minute, when Baena won possession and Rodri, Yamal and Olmo combined to make a chance for Ruiz. It all happened too fast for France.

In the moments before the second goal, Olmo moved through yards of space where the French midfield should have been. Seconds later Porro passed the ball urgently to the feet of Olmo and slipped the return past Mike Maignan. Deschamps tried to change with Désiré Doué and Rayan Cherki, but it was too late. France were in the trap, and there was no escape.

A special display 💫 pic.twitter.com/gCaBU8kmpw

It’s a dream come true. Truly it was my biggest dream. Very, very happy for the team. From the start to the end we played a great game. We did everything possible today to be able to go through.

We knew we were facing a very, very tough team and we did everything so well. This is a team effort, it’s not about me. I just want to say congratulations to everybody for producing such a great performance.

Of course we knew this [having the ball], was one of the things that would take us closer to the final. Their counter attack is their strong suit and stopping that was key.

We are all really disappointed by the result but mostly by the performance because we needed our top players to perform today and they didn’t do it. It wasn’t one or two that were missing, all of them were missing.

Gary Neville had the following to say: “Absolutely incredible performance from Spain.

“I’m going to bring it back to my favourite topic which is full-backs and the biggest differential between both teams can be told through stories of the full-backs.

“[Pedro] Porro and [Marc] Cucurella have been absolutely outstanding against those French wide players and Lucas Digne really struggled against Lamine Yamal. We talked before the game about if he can turn up in the biggest moments and today he has done.

“We’re a little bit in shock here because we all had France [to win] and they have been blown away.”

7/8 - Among European nations to contest at least two, Spain have the best progression rate in major tournament semi-final ties (World Cup/EURO), progressing from seven of their eight (88%).Dominance. pic.twitter.com/idrHLD6NgK

Spain are into the World Cup final at the expense of France after a comprehensive team performance. They were collectively better than the tournament favourites across the pitch, and too many of France’s attacking players suffered from off nights. In their defence, there was little platform to play from, with Rodri and Fabian Ruiz imperious in midfield, throwing a blanket over their opponents. There will be no third World Cup final for Deschamps, who will soon retire.

England or Argentina await Spain in New Jersey.

French hearts are broken as Spain book their place in the World Cup final 🇪🇸 pic.twitter.com/o67kAqt78O

You know it has been a bad night when Marc Cucurella is flicking the ball over your head and picking it up the other side. Real Madrid’s new left-back has been magnificent. Dembele with another shot saved by Simon. Mbappe shoots well over from distance, and De La Fuente begins the high fives with his coaching staff.

Spain threaten to score a third goal with Nico Williams facing up Kounde and dribbling into the penalty area. His left-footed shot with a tight angle hits the side netting.

France go on the attack, but Dembele’s shot is straight at the goalkeeper Simon.

Cucurella punches the air after winning a crucial challenge on Mbappe in the box, and then Kone’s inswinging cross creeps wide. Spain can see the finish line, seven minutes of added time to play.

France have another free-kick near the left-corner of the box, and Mbappe might be lining up a shot. His effort balloons over the bar though, and the grimace as he turns away says it all.

At the other end, Yamal turns Hernandez and looks to be dragged down, but on second look the Spain winger threw himself to the floor.

This referee really does not want to book Lamine Yamal.

Dembele has had a stinker, and the Ballon d’Or winner has just skewed a free-kick into the box at shin height. Spain are cruising through the final, it’s scarcely believable how comfortable it has been. Has had the feel of a mid-season home banker, and the flat atmosphere in Dallas has played into that.

France corner, after Simon smothers at the feet of Hernandez. Or perhaps not, the referee overturns the decision and Spain have a goal kick. They make two more changes: Porro and Baena off, Nico Williams and Marcos Llorente on. Two strong runners with space to counter-attack into.

Was that France’s chance to kickstart an unlikely comeback? Simon playing sweeper-keeper again, but his header falls to the feet of Doue around 40 yards out. Declined to take an early touch, tried to carry the ball forward to set himself, but Simon recovered into his box to block Doue’s tame effort.

Spain with another double change: Pedri and Mikel Merino on for Fabian Ruiz and Dani Olmo, who were fantastic. With Merino on, shall we pencil him in for the Spain third ahead of time?

Mbappe’s best moment of the game, receiving the ball deep and driving forward at pace, eventually hacked down by Lamine Yamal. No yellow card, which is very lenient indeed. At touch of the Clasico in a World Cup semi-final there.

The man’s shamelessness is beyond the pale, but there is no doubting Gianni Infantino’s stamina. Having spent six weeks criss-crossing North America, he has travelled to this semi-final in Dallas straight from Doha, eight time zones away. The Fifa president was in Qatar to pay respects to Sheikh Tamim bin Hamad Al-Thani after the death of his father. Qatar, not without coincidence, has also been providing Infantino’s private jet.

Ferran Torres is on for Spain, replaces Mikel Oyarzabal. Just a question of remaining professional and rigorous defensively for Spain. They beat France in the semi-finals of the Euros two summers ago, and they are on course to do the same. Doue’s pass towards Mbappe drifts behind for a Spain goal kick.

France rolling the dice with a double sub: Olise and Digne off, Cherki and Hernandez on. The latter change feels as much about taking Digne off than introducing Theo Hernandez.

Time for another hydration break. France have 20 minutes plus stoppage time to ensure the Deschamps era does not end in a third-place play-off. This has been the performance of the tournament from Spain so far.

Jubilant scenes back home in Madrid, where Mbappe with return after the tournament.

That was at least a moment of danger, Mbappe finding space at the edge of the box and his deflected shot whistled wide of the post. It was good defending from Cucurella to make the partial block.

Amusing moment in the stadium when Jerry Jones, Dallas Cowboys owner, is suddenly on the big stadium screens and the crowd boos. But the camera shot on our monitors is Gianni Infantino, who is looking up at the screen to check it isn’t him being booed. And smiles when he sees that it is, in fact, Jones.

Tchouameni with a glancing header from a corner, but it is thin gruel from a French perspective. Doue’s pass towards Digne is too heavy and Porro slides in to win the ball. Give the ball to Mbappe and pray might need to be the strategy.

The story of these World Cup semi-finals could yet be written by Tottenham full-backs. With Djed Spence potentially the man to try to thwart Lionel Messi in Atlanta, Pedro Porro lashes in the strike here in Dallas that promises to put Spain out of sight. An extraordinary few minutes of dominance by the European champions.

The ball is in France’s net again, after a cracking finish by Yamal, but the flag is raised for offside. Digne living life on the edge again, and France are on the brink of a collapse, unless they drag a performance from nowhere.

That goal was on the cards for a few minutes with Spain keeping the ball, but in the end it was too easy. Porro with a pass into the feet of Olmo, and he continues his run to receive the return pass. France’s defence parts, and Porro keeps his composure to pass the ball beyond Maignan.

France on the brink here. Olmo had run into yards of space moments earlier in the midfield. A few seconds later, Pedro Porro popped it into Olmo and ran through three French defenders for the return. No-one went with him. Porro channelling prime Postecoglou free-attacking vibes.

INCREDIBLE 🔥Spain one-two their way through the France defense and Pedro Porro doubles his country's lead... pic.twitter.com/DFGCLMzFUC

It is indeed Doue on for Barcola. Spain continuing to move the ball through France’s shape so easily, the blue shirts are chasing shadows. Rodri and Ruiz keep tempting them in and the ball moves on.

More promising from France, but Barcola’s touch is heavy as he tries to beat Porro on the outside and Simon gathers.

Another France change on the way, the third of the game, and Doue is ready. I think that might be for Dembele, even if the hierarchy of the squad suggests it will be Barcola.

Bradley Barcola’s speed bails out Lucas Digne who is having a torrid time against Yamal. Dembele then clumps a pass straight out for a Spain goal kick, France just cannot find their range at all in the last third.

Oyarzabal miscued a backheel, looking for Olmo, but the ball stayed in his path and the Spain striker fired over on the swivel with his left foot. Usain Bolt and Noah Lyles are watching on from the stands.

Rodri and Fabian Ruiz has been superb in the middle of the park, and they are continuing to control the game. Both looks so unhurried, while France’s football is frantic. Yamal spinds behind the France defence, but the flag goes up for offside.

Laporte reads play well to intercept as Olise looks for the run of Mbappe with a curling pass from the right touchline. Excellent defending then from Cucurella after Olise rolls inside, stuck to him like glue. France’s forwards need to release the ball and have fewer touches, Spain do not offer that sort of time.

Some early posssession for France, but deep in their own half.

Manu Kone replaces Rabiot in midfield, a reflection of his performance and the fact he was on a booking.

Patrick Vieira said: “The French team didn’t turn up. They need to play with more intensity. It’s comfortable for the Spanish to control the game. France can’t be worse than the first half.”

Neville said about the penalty: “Only the elite top players do this. Lamine waits for Digne to turn and then he sprints. It’s exceptional.”

Keane said: “It’s still poor defending. Spain look sharper. Off the ball they are working so hard, working in packs.”

2 - Only two sides have progressed from a FIFA World Cup semi-final having trailed at half time: Argentina v Italy - 1990Croatia v England - 2018Uphill. pic.twitter.com/3z5fkurDXP

An excellent team performance from Spain so far: solid defending, snuffing out counter-attacks, keeping the ball and carrying a threat themselves. France have just been too loose technically, unable to sustain attacks with any continuity. Their midfield axis of Tchouameni and Rabiot has been found wanting, and the forwards have been inaccurate. With Saliba off injured too, Deschamps’s team have a lot of work to do, but we know they and Mbappe can flick the switch.

Rabiot with a foul on Fabian Ruiz, and he is walking a tightrope on a yellow card. There will be six minutes of added time. Dembele with another loose France pass as Olise looked for a one two.

There was an odd moment that preceded that, with VAR appearing to overreach its remit by overturning a France free-kick.

Strange moment there from the El Salvador referee who originally gave a foul on Dembélé by Ruiz and then changed his mind. Probably on advice of assistant. VAR couldn’t intervene and we ended up with a dropped ball. France still struggling to break this Spanish line. Spain have been excellent. So judicious in possession. They have kept the big France attackers out of their area and thus out of dangerous areas. France have just given the ball away too much.

Unai Simon with a critical piece of goalkeeping, sweeping up behind his defence with Kylian Mbappe running off the shoulder of Curbasi. Well outside his area, Simon went to ground to win the sliding challenge. A second later, and he was off. From the set-piece, Simon almost undoes his good work by fumbling the ball but play is stopped because of an Olmo head injury.

France looking a little ragged, they have been untidy in possession in the first few passes after winning it back. Pedro Porro sends a dipping shot from long range over the bar.

Then Spain almost score one of the goals of the tournament! Maignan with a terrible pass straight to Baena, he passed the ball to Rodri who found Yamal, and he played a delightful one-two with Olmo. Fabian Ruiz thought he had a tap in, but he was denied by a last-ditch Upamecano challenge.

Baena in behind the France defence and he pushed the ball beyond the goalkeeper Maignan, but the flag went up for offside. Could well have been a second penalty had he stayed onside.

Marc Cucurella will play the rest of the game on a booking, dragging back Michael Olise. Spain quickly restore control though, they look quite comfortable at the moment.

Mikel Oyarzabal is your classic low-key World Cup everyman hero. That said, his record for Spain is sensational. He is a one-in-two goalscorer for his country. His wages at Real Socieded probably a third of what the big guns earn - and in the case of the elite like Mbappe, an even bigger differential.

France have another setback to deal with: William Saliba is down holding his back and cannot continue. The Arsenal defender has been nursing back pain throughout the tournament and it has got the better of him here. Crystal Palace’s Maxence Lacroix replaces him. There have been reports of Saliba needing a procedure once the tournament is over.

Play has now resumed after the game’s first hydration break. The pause in play might not be good news for Spain.

This was Gary Neville’s view on the penalty: “Lucas Digne has to be more aware. He’s got to understand that Lamine Yamal is going to gamble.”

Terrific penalty, and for the first time in the tournament France are behind in a game. This sets up the game a treat.

Mikel Oyarzabal scores from the spot and Spain lead France! 🇪🇸 pic.twitter.com/pJManVOZf3

Oyarzabal is going to take it, against Maignan.

Yamal vs Digne was the match-up that always looked advantageous for Spain, and so it has proved. A strange incident, with Digne controlling the ball on his chest in his own penalty, looking to turn and volley a clearance on the swivel. Yamal got there first, and Digne whacks him in the thigh. Surely no chance of a VAR reprieve, even if the ball hit the upper part of Yamal’s arm.

I find that penalty hard to give if Spence’s is not given against Norway. Yamal knew the contact was coming and threw himself into it. No different to Spence anticipating the contact.

The Oscar Bobb foul on Spence was a case of the former moving into the space occupied by the latter, and knocking him over. It doesn’t matter whether Spence stopped and allowed Bobb to run into him, the point is that he had got there first to occupy the space that Bobb came into. Same with the Yamal penalty. Yamal moved into the space that Digne had swung his boot into and took the contact.

France’s most dangerous moment so far, a sweeping counter-attack. Dembele curled a fantastic pass behind Curbasi for the run of Mbappe, but Porro and others recovered at pace to just about deter Mbappe from a clean strike at goal. Three white shirts converged on him. The ball ended up with Olise on the right, and his cross was deflected behind. Spain defend, but France keep the pressure on.

That Dembélé pass to Mbappe was an example of how willing France are to risk the ball compared to Spain. It’s fast and furious when they go forward. Mbappe had it and then he lost it and then he won it back.

In a role reversal of what you might expect, Michael Olise has fouled Rodri. That was a little bit naughty from the France player, clamping his studs on the back of the ankle. Lucky to escape without a yellow card, even if there was not gratuitous force behind the challenge.

Very deliberate and precise passing from Spain. Not really putting a foot wrong. They have had 61 per cent of the in-play possession. France looking to break the lines much more swiftly when they get possession and making more mistakes or giving the ball away more often. The free-kick conceded by Rabiot was a good chance for Spain that they wasted immediately.

Laporte ushers the ball behind for a goal kick as France looked for a long ball over the top to Kounde. Arsene Wenger and Xavi are in the row behind Infantino in the stands. Both teams backing themselves to pass out under pressure, there has already been a line-splitting ball from France goalkeeper Maignan.

The free-kick takes a while because the referee has left his spray in the dressing room, and he runs across to the fourth official for a replacement. Baena shoots straight into the wall, before Yamal lifts a delightful cross to the back post. Kounde did ever so well to head clear, but that was dangerous.

Very good defensive intervention from Rodri, after Kounde carried the ball forward and tried to release Dembele on the inside with a cute pass. Then Olise turns into space but his through ball was intercepted when there was green grass to find the run of Mbappe. Cut out, then Spain win a free-kick on the edge of France’s box. Rabiot stood on the top of Olmo’s left boot. Game suddenly became quite stretched in that passage of play.

Spain being very patient on the ball in the France half, but once they lose possession France attack at pace. Barcola skips beyond Porro who goes sliding in, missing man and ball, and the winger’s cross deflects behind for a corner. Headed clear, then Rabiot slices a volley wide.

France in a very clear 4-4-2 shape when they are defending, leaving Olise and Mbappe high. Barcola and Dembele will have to be diligent defensively in the wide midfield positions. A couple of early giveaways from both teams, and Yamal brings the ball forward for Spain and helps them establish possession in the France half.

No quarter given by either side in the early exchanges, Cucurella keeps his legs closed to prevent Dembele from nutmegging him. Unai Simon tries to attract pressure from Mbappe, and his clipped ball forward is cut out by Rabiot. Then Spain settle into a spell of possession.

Spain, playing in white with maroon shorts, kick off.

That marks the 10th anniversary of the Nice terrorists attacks. More of a moment’s silence in fact, the referee blows his whistle very early.

Time for the soaring, if lyrically bellicose, La Marseillaise, followed by Spain’s wordless national anthem. As Trigger might have said, it’s the karaoke version.

Clearly we are coming up against a top team. It is their third consecutive World Cup semi-final, and these things are not gifted to you. We’re going to find out the strength that they have, both collectively and individually, but we will meet that with our strengths. I hope that our collective efforts overcome theirs, that is the level we intend to reach.

This World Cup has attracted local fans – and in particular US nationals who moved to the country as adults for work. At the AT&T Stadium in Dallas there were fans of France and Spain who did not have a direct connection to either of the two countries, but had fallen in love with the teams.

Manvith Reddy – in the France shirt - and his friend Ritesh Reddy, supporting Spain, are both 26 and originally from Hyderabad, India. Both are software engineers in San Francisco and. “I have been watching football since I was a kid,” says Manvith. “To be able to go to a World Cup semi-final and support France – it’s something people dream of doing.”

Like Manvith, Ritesh spent $2,000 [£1,495] on his World Cup semi-final ticket. “I am a Barcelona fan and Pedri is my faavourite player. I have seen Spain before and this was just too good an opportunity to miss.”

Also software engineers in San Francisco, Dagim Abebe [left] and his friend Kidist Alemu [right] decided to pay the Fifa prices for what they saw as a once in a lifetime opportunity. Both originally from Ethiopia, Dagim, 25, said the prices were “painful” but that he had always followed France as his international side. “A World Cup is only ever four years so it is worth it,” he said.

Kidist, 25, said that she had been a football fan her whole life, although the World Cup was not necessarily a topic of conversation among co-worker. “I had to get them interested but people are hearing about it on the news and on social media.”

The player with the most cut-through with the new-to-soccer US audience? “Haaland,” said Kidist. “His social media has captured him in an interesting way. He is very relatable. He is silly but at the same time a very strong athlete. He doesn’t seem a typical footballer and the combination of the two makes him standout.”

Ian Rodriguez, 21, lives in Dallas, Texas, and works in a restaurant. His family are originally from Mexico. He was in the Spain shirt today with his father having surprised the whole family with tickets for the game. A committed football fan he saw England beat the Netherlands in Dortmund at Euro 2024.

“Just the big draw of Lamine Yamal attacts a lot of people to Spain,” he said. Spain have not played such attractive football as France at this World Cup – Americans want to see goals. Spain are very slow and they chip away at an opponent. I think they will win.”

The below statistic speaks to the fact Barcelona have had the upper hand over Real Madrid domestically. Sam Wallace explored why Mbappe is curiously unloved by Real Madrid supporters, despite the fact he has scored plenty of goals. Paris St-Germain winning consecutive Champions League – the trophy Real regard as theirs – since Mbappe left the French club has not helped matters. Now there is the prospect of Mbappe returning to the Spanish capital after scoring the goal that knocks his country of employment out of a World Cup.

11 - Tonight will be the 11th time Lamine Yamal and Kylian Mbappé have been on opposite sides in a match for club and country - Yamal has won eight of those 10 games.Showdown. pic.twitter.com/iXUtRzD2e4

We will not enjoy many more sightings of ITV’s Brooklyn studio, and the man who won a Golden Globe for his portrayal of Logan Roy in Succession has graced it with his presence.

If they win the World Cup for a third time this Sunday, they’ll hope they don’t wake up to find this was all a dream...

🇫🇷 | France supporters in Dallas before semi-final match vs Spain. Allez les bleus…👊🏼🔵#FIFAWorldCup pic.twitter.com/izHoxYLA4Z

But you can do so here using our predictor tool. In truth, the tournament has gone to form; France, Spain, England and Argentina were the front four in the betting markets before a ball was kicked.

Manu Koné can consider himself unlucky to drop out of France’s midfield, replaced by Aurelien Tchouaméni. Didier Deschamps has also opted for Bradley Barcola instead of Désiré Doué on the left. I suspect that is in anticipation of Spain having their share of possession, exposing themselves to Barcola’s pace on the break.

No surprises for Spain. Pedri stays on the bench, as does Mikel Merino who scored their winning goal in each of the last two rounds.

France XI: Maignan; Digne, Saliba, Upamecano, Koundé; Rabiot, Tchouaméni; Barcola, Olise, Dembélé; Mbappé

Spain XI: Unai Simon; Porro, Curbasi, Laporte, Cucurella; Rodri, Fabian Ruiz; Yamal, Olmo, Beana; Oyarzabal

Whether you use your eyes or the underlying data, France and Spain look like the two best teams at this World Cup but only one of them will play in Sunday’s final in New Jersey.

France are the tournament’s top goalscorers with 16 goals in six matches, while European champions Spain have the best defence.

France have certainly played as a team at this tournament, but their great strength is a luxurious level of individual quality across a front four of Desire Doue, Michael Olise, Ousmane Dembele and Kylian Mbappe. Spain rely more on their collective patterns of play and a distinctive style which is very much their own.

Most opponents at this World Cup have sat off France and hoped to limit the damage, but Spain will not approach the game in that way which should make this an absorbing contest. Spain will want to hide the ball from France’s best players and ask questions of a defence which has been relatively untroubled.

However, the more possession Spain have, the higher up the pitch their centre-backs Aymeric Laporte and Pau Curbasi will be, which could leave them vulnerable to a fleet-footed France attack in transition.

If Spain are to hurt France, Lamine Yamal against either Theo Hernandez or Lucas Digne looks the most likely avenue. Left-back is the one position in France’s team in which they do not have an elite option.

Spain have been utterly dominant for long stretches of their matches, but have not converted that control into goals in the manner of France, and that could well be the difference. Mbappe is currently the tournament’s joint top scorer on eight goals, level with Lionel Messi and one behind Argentine in the all-time list of World Cup top scorers.

Messi, and England, will be watching this closely and it ought to be a cracker in Dallas.

#2324. Recoba: "All'Inter momenti indimenticabili, ho realizzato un sogno. Rimpianti? No perché..."

Recoba: "All'Inter momenti indimenticabili, ho realizzato un sogno. Rimpianti? No perché..."

Installa la nostra App gratuita e accedi più velocemente alle notizie

Mentre navighi nell'app, scorri il dito da sinistra a destra dello schermo per tornare alle pagine precedenti

Scegli Fcinter1908.it come tuo sito preferito su Google: clicca quiAlvaro Recoba ha rilasciato una lunga intervista a Flashscore nel corso della quale ha parlato anche del suo periodo all'Inter:

"Nessun allenatore mi ha lasciato un'impronta tattica. Quelli che mi hanno influenzato sono stati i tecnici che mi parlavano direttamente e mi dicevano sempre la verità. Mi piacevano quelli che facevano così, perché potevano parlare con me e dirmi le cose in faccia. Potrei nominare quasi tutti gli allenatori che ho avuto. Con chi ho avuto il miglior rapporto? Ho lavorato con Marcelo Gallardo nella fase finale della mia carriera da giocatore. Lui era appena agli inizi come allenatore e avevamo quasi la stessa età, quindi siamo andati molto d'accordo. Ho avuto allenatori come Marcello Lippi, con cui mi trovavo molto bene, e Cuper, con cui avevo un buon rapporto. C'era Novellino, quando lo ebbi a Venezia, Simoni, il mio primo allenatore all'Inter, e Alvaro Gutierrez, con cui ho lavorato al Nacional verso la fine della mia carriera. Ce n'erano altri con quelle qualità. Erano persone che ti parlavano direttamente, ti aiutavano, ti facevano crescere e apprezzavano il fatto che tu le aiutassi a tua volta. È così che vedo la vita ed è così che vedo il calcio. Credo che siamo allenatori, ma anche educatori. Dobbiamo lasciare qualcosa."

Guardando indietro, qual è il ricordo complessivo della tua esperienza all'Inter? Com'è stato vivere a Milano e far parte di quell'ambiente?

"Mi ha dato tantissimo. Mi ha dato la possibilità di guadagnarmi da vivere facendo ciò che inizialmente era un hobby: giocare a calcio. Tutti vorrebbero giocare a calcio. Come dico sempre, se non avessi giocato da professionista, avrei comunque giocato. Mi sarei considerato un calciatore mentre giocavo con gli amici. Che cosa mi ha dato l'Inter? La possibilità di guadagnarmi da vivere con uno dei miei hobby, il calcio. Ero pagato per fare qualcosa che avrei fatto comunque, magari a un livello più basso o con i miei amici, ma sarebbe sempre stato un hobby. Per me calciare un pallone è un hobby. Mi ha dato la possibilità di guadagnarmi da vivere, di garantire stabilità economica alla mia famiglia, di guadagnare denaro e di aiutare mio padre, mia madre e i miei fratelli. Oltre a questo, l'Inter mi ha permesso di conoscere una nuova cultura e mi ha regalato tanti amici. Mi ha dato un club in cui, ora che ho 50 anni, la gente si ricorda ancora di me ogni volta che vado in Italia e mi accoglie con grande gioia. È questo ciò che resta. Alla fine ho avuto il privilegio di giocare in una delle squadre più grandi del mondo. Come ho detto prima, avere la possibilità di aiutare la mia famiglia e continuare a farlo mi ha dato tutto. E non solo l'Inter, ma anche il Nacional. Trascorrere così tanti anni in un club importante come l'Inter non è qualcosa che capita a tutti e io ho avuto il privilegio di esserci per molti anni."

Molti dicono che avevi il talento per diventare uno dei più grandi giocatori di sempre della Serie A, ma che gli infortuni ti hanno frenato...

"Non lo so. Non ho mai avuto infortuni gravi, sai? Incredibilmente, ho sempre avuto problemi di poco conto: uno stiramento al bicipite femorale, un polpaccio contratto e così via. Non so se avrei potuto fare di più. Non rimpiango mai ciò che ho fatto o ciò che non ho fatto. Capisci? Perché credo che il rimpianto sia la cosa peggiore. All'epoca, quando giocavo, credevo di fare del mio meglio, o il meglio che era nelle mie possibilità. Forse avrei potuto fare di più, ma come possiamo saperlo? Non si può tornare indietro. Quel che è stato è stato. Ho avuto una carriera molto bella. Avrebbe potuto essere migliore, così come avrebbe potuto essere peggiore. Avrebbe potuto essere molto peggiore. Ho avuto il privilegio di rappresentare il mio Paese, di giocare un Mondiale, di vestire la maglia di squadre importanti e di fare cose importanti nel calcio. Quindi forse avrei potuto fare di più, ma avrei anche potuto fare di meno. Non rimpiango ciò che è successo o ciò che ho fatto in campo. E, naturalmente, come ho detto, c'è sempre qualcuno che può dirti: 'No, ma avresti potuto fare di più'. Sì, ma avrei anche potuto fare di meno. Dipende da come la si guarda. Ero sempre soddisfatto. Forse quello era il problema. Mi accontentavo di ciò che avevo, magari per il modo in cui calciavo il pallone o per questo e quello. Era anche un'epoca in cui non avevi tutti intorno a te a dirti di fare lavoro preventivo o allenamento della forza. Non era come oggi, sai?"

Quale punizione o gol del periodo all'Inter ricordi con più affetto? E c'è un trofeo o un momento che rimpiangi o che avresti voluto vivere diversamente?

"Ci sono stati così tanti gol bellissimi che è difficile sceglierne uno. Il fatto è che molti sono arrivati con conclusioni dalla distanza, dribbling, tiri dalla linea di metà campo o palloni colpiti al volo. Ci sono stati tantissimi gol. Non ce ne sono stati molti brutti (ride). Alla fine valevano tutti allo stesso modo. Con l'Inter ho vissuto momenti indimenticabili, vincendo lo Scudetto, la Coppa Italia e quella che oggi sarebbe l'Europa League."

#2323. Lazio, tre punti sulla seconda: in Europa solo l’Arsenal tiene il passo

I Gunners precedono il Manchester City; negli altri tre campionati citati il distacco dalla seconda è di due punti.

La Lazio ha tre punti di vantaggio sulla seconda in classifica: nessuna capolista dei cinque maggiori campionati europei presenta un margine superiore. L’unica squadra che eguaglia i biancocelesti è l’Arsenal, anch’esso avanti di tre punti rispetto alla propria inseguitrice. Negli altri campionati presi in esame, il distacco tra prima e seconda è di due punti.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Il dato riguarda il vantaggio della squadra al primo posto su quella immediatamente alle sue spalle. Sotto questo aspetto la Lazio condivide il margine più ampio tra le capoliste considerate: i suoi tre punti di vantaggio trovano un solo equivalente, quello dell’Arsenal. Barcellona, PSG e Dortmund guidano invece le rispettive classifiche con un punto di margine in meno rispetto ai biancocelesti.

Il riferimento è alle prime due posizioni nei cinque maggiori campionati europei. Per la Lazio il confronto mette quindi insieme il suo distacco sulla seconda e quelli registrati in Spagna, Francia, Germania e Inghilterra. Il primato dei biancocelesti è richiamato anche in un contenuto dedicato a Reja, che dichiara: «Vedere la Lazio prima mi riempie di gioia. Non deve porsi limiti».

In Spagna, secondo quanto riportato da Sportmediaset.mediaset.it,  il Barcellona è a più due sull’Atletico Madrid. La formazione che occupa la prima posizione precede dunque la seconda con un vantaggio inferiore di un punto a quello della Lazio. Il confronto tra le due capoliste mostra margini diversi: tre punti per i biancocelesti, due per il Barcellona.

La stessa distanza si registra in Francia, dove il PSG conserva due punti sul Lille. Anche in Bundesliga il Dortmund precede il Friburgo di due punti. Barcellona, PSG e Dortmund hanno così il medesimo vantaggio sulle rispettive inseguitrici, mentre la Lazio dispone di un punto in più di margine sulla seconda. Il quadro dei distacchi nei tre campionati è quindi uniforme: ciascuna delle loro capoliste è avanti di due punti.

#2322. Brekalo non ha dubbi: "Modric è il calcio, un orgoglio per tutti i croati"

Le bellissime parole di Josip Brekalo, centrocampista dell'Herta Berlino, che in passato ha giocato anche in Italia con Torino e Fiorentina ha rilasciato un'intervista ai microfoni di Gianluca Di Marzio. Tra i tanti temi toccati non poteva mancare quello su Luka Modric, esempio per la sua Croazia e per tutti gli amanti del calcio:

"Lui è tutto per il nostro calcio. Luka dà consigli a tutti ed è un punto di riferimento per ognuno di noi. Ha vinto il Pallone d'Oro, una cosa incredibile per un Paese di 3-4 milioni di abitanti. Siamo molto orgogliosi. Per me non è una sorpresa che a 41 anni giochi ancora nel Milan... Lo conosco molto bene e so che professionista è e come vive il calcio ogni giorno. Si può imparare molto da lui."

"Una vittoria incredibile, la miglior partita giocata in questa stagione, ha commentato il croato nella notte del derby alla Cbs, Stiamo crescendo come squadra, ci sono nuove idee di gioco. Voglio aiutare la squadra e i più giovani a crescere".

#2321. Juventus Napoli, le formazioni ufficiali: Hojlund ha la febbre, Lucca dal 1′

Juventus (4-3-3)Vicario; Kalulu, Bremer, Lucumi, Cabal; Douglas Luiz, Koopmeiners, McKennie; Alajbegovic, Woltemade, Kolo Muani. All. Spalletti. A disposizione: Pinsoglio, Neto, Gatti, Kelly, Zhegrova, Boga, Ekhator, Cambiaso, Rugani, Sarr.

Napoli (4-3-3): Meret; Di Lorenzo, Marianucci, Rafa Marin, Olivera; Anguissa, De Bruyne, McTominay; Politano, Lucca, Lang. All. Allegri. A disposizione: Contini, Spinelli, Gilmour, Rrahmani, Barido, Giovane, Vergara, Beukema, Pereyra, Chiummariello, Melnyk, Lieto

#2315. At the moment of reckoning, England’s old anxiety took hold

All the years that have passed since those red shirts on a summer’s day at a long-gone Wembley Stadium – the humiliations, the occasional triumph, the plentiful servings of self-pity. For a short while in Atlanta it felt like that epoch might finally be ending and all it came down to was managing the last five minutes and whatever the beleaguered referee might add.

A place at a World Cup final was within England’s grasp, a mere 60 years since the last. But just as it loomed into view, already the players and their manager were tracing an old path. The man currently in charge of the England team might be German, the players might be of a generation finessed under bold new coaching ideas, but once more a familiar anxiety took hold. England were minutes from the prize. Yet team and manager were already obeying an old instinct to sabotage the moment.

England had been in retreat long before Lionel Messi served up for Enzo Fernández the goal that would level this World Cup semi-final, and then the second for Lautaro Martínez ,which would propel Argentina forward. Of course, Messi is Messi, and this Argentina team exist to exert his will on games by any means necessary. Yet, as ever, at the moment of reckoning, England demonstrated all the resistance of damp cardboard.

Thomas Tuchel once again reverted to the deep defensive position. Once again, the adapted five-man back line sat on the edge of their area. Against Mexico in the last 16, Tuchel was dismissive of any suggestion he had invited pressure, asking what other option he had with just 10 men. Against Norway in the quarter-finals he did the same, once Erling Haaland was off the pitch. Against Argentina, a team accustomed to playing with it all on the line, such a move was fatal.

Argentina are ahead! 😱A relentless spell of pressure sees Alexis Mac Allister hit the post before ANOTHER Lionel Messi assist sets up Lautaro Martinez to make it 2-1. pic.twitter.com/kVoNouFgKZ

Immediately the supporters in the stands in Atlanta, and in the streets and front rooms of England, recognised the mistake. The punditocracy on the BBC saw it, too. Former players Wayne Rooney and Joe Hart have been here before and treated this defeat much like revisiting a trauma. For the rest of us it was hard to miss: like staring into the face of the England team over the decades that have passed since 1966.

"The wait and the pain goes on for English football." 💔England's dream of reaching a first men's World Cup final since 1966 is over as Argentina make it six wins from six semi-final appearances 👏 pic.twitter.com/AEMHqqcYfI

Tuchel said that Argentina’s tactical changes as he saw them – two wide wingers, two central strikers – forced his hand. So too, he seemed to suggest, did the attitude of his players. He talked about them failing to be “active” as he called it, engaging their opposition and taking the game to them. But by this point those on the outside could see it more clearly than him.

This kind of retreat, to a greater or lesser degree, was the default position of so many England teams. England led against Brazil in 2002, Croatia in 2018, Italy in 2021. Sir Gareth Southgate, a man more criticised for a reluctance to make substitutions, never went into reverse quite as spectacularly as this. The record is now two World Cup semi-finals, and two European Championship finals in the last five tournaments. But that final step, or the penultimate one at the World Cup, is the primary English problem.

The recriminations from Atlanta will last for years, and as for the precious opportunity – who knows when it will come again?

Tuchel is part of this unfortunate history now, and defeats like this often suck reputations into the void. Afterwards he breezily turned over the page to Euro 2028, before England complete Saturday’s third-place play-off, although one feels that moving on will be more difficult than that.

After Fernández’s 85th-minute equaliser, Messi found the substitute Martínez at the back post for a winner seven minutes later. “There was blood in the water and we went for it,” Lionel Scaloni, the Argentina head coach, said. His team, to their credit, never gave up the chase.

They pursued that equaliser after Anthony Gordon’s 55th-minute goal, a fine finish after England launched a quick attack down the right that ended with Morgan Rogers sweeping the ball to the back post. England had just 12 per cent of the ball from that moment until Martínez’s 92nd-minute goal.

ENGLAND HAVE THE OPENER! 🤩Anthony Gordon puts the Three Lions ahead in the World Cup semi-final! pic.twitter.com/5fBlRcf1oI

This Argentina team are very hard to love, but you have to admire the tenacity with which they keep going. Around Messi is a fairly mixed bag of young talents and old boys clinging on. Their commitment to the cause, however, is not in doubt. They handle the moments of adversity better than most.

As for England, in the minutes that followed Gordon’s goal, Tuchel and his players seemed to make up their minds that what had sufficed for Norway and Mexico would work for Argentina.

He brought off Gordona after 72 minutes for Ezri Konsa and then Dan Burn and Nico O’Reilly came on for Reece James and Declan Rice. In the meantime, the game was following an inevitable path. There were nine attempts on the England goal, including the two that went in, after Gordon scored. Alexis Mac Allister somehow clipped a post with a header. Jordan Pickford made one of the saves of his life from the substitute Nicolás González. A post was struck again by Mac Allister before the second goal.

In other words: this goal was coming. This Argentina have some fine players, including Messi, Julián Alvarez, Fernández, Emiliano Martínez – but they are so much more than the sum of their parts. The game finished with the 38-year-old Nicolás Otamendi on the pitch. So too the MLS midfielder Rodrigo De Paul, as well as the right-back Gonzalo Montiel, 29, who did not make the grade on loan at Nottingham Forest.

“They were raised in an environment where they feared nothing,” Scaloni said of his players. “They don’t feel the weight on their shoulders.” In those painful last moments, Tuchel tried to reverse his earlier mistake by sending on Ivan Toney and Marcus Rashford, but the momentum had slipped away. England were beaten.

It had been intense from the start with the Argentine cynicism as high as ever. Elliot Anderson was targeted and eventually booked when Djed Spence nudged a charging Messi into the midfielder’s path. Spence was superb for much of the game, including a recovery tackle on Giuliano Simeone after the Gordon goal.

WHAT A TACKLE FROM DJED SPENCE 👊 pic.twitter.com/kyrHBS0tzX

At the critical moment when Messi prepared to cross for the Martínez goal, however, Spence seemed to feel a bolt of pain and could not react in time.

It was a gripping game, even without a single shot on target from either side in the first half. Ismail Elfath, the US referee for whom this game felt impossible at times, was slow to book Argentina players. They seemed to know who to target, in particular Jude Bellingham and Pickford. Bellingham had some fine moments in the first half but he struggled after the break.

England’s goal came 10 minutes after half-time, when the momentum had been with Argentina. Nicolás Tagliafico only just stopped Harry Kane’s long ball forward. From there Rice fed Rogers and the cross was perfect. At the back post, Gordon slipped past his man and guided it home, right-footed, at the back post.

In the aftermath England seemed to retreat. Tuchel said that he did not believe in a curse that afflicts England in the closing stages of such big knockout games. Of course, he was seeing it first hand for the first time – which, for the rest of us, is sadly not the case.

Thomas Müller: "I cannot believe and understand how England approached this game after they're leading. I cannot understand how they invited Argentina to play one cross after the other in perfect crossing positions" pic.twitter.com/Hm35pZfRwf

🚨 WATCH: Police rescue an Argentina fan in London after he was surrounded by England fans pic.twitter.com/ukDjubWuEV

[Reporter: Did the better team win?] “I don’t know. I thought we deserved to be 1-0 up. The momentum changed after that. They had nothing to lose and we suddenly played like we had a lot to lose. They took more and more risks and turned the game around in the last 20 minutes.

[Argentina had 88 per cent possession after Gordon’s goal] “It felt like that.” [What went wrong?] “We didn’t have enough possession to escape the pressure. We tried everything but couldn’t get hold of the ball. It’s like you’re dying a slow death. It started straight away after the goal and is the reason basically that we lost.”

“We were the better team until we cored then the mindset shifted, they took a lot of risks and have a lot of quality.

[You took Gordon off for Konsa at 71 minutes and sat deep] “We sat deep from when we scored the goal. Not after the substitutions. I take the responsibility. It’s easy to be a coach after the game. The result tells the story. No one can say what would have happened with other substitutions. I thought we needed a back five with all the crossing.

“We didn’t stop the second line coming through the gaps. We were not physical enough in these moments. It’s not about structure. You can play in any structure deep, you can defend in any structure if you’re active but we were too passive.

[Harry said he’d been here before, 1-0 up in the 2018 semi-final, 1-0 up in the Euro 2020 final] “I can’t speak about that I was not part of that.

“No regrets, we gave everything, that’s the bottom line. We gave everything. It’s difficult to accept because we hate to lose. We were very close. For the overall summary we have to wait a bit and digest it.

"I take the responsibility"Thomas Tuchel reacts as England's World Cup dream comes to an end following defeat to Argentina...🎙️ @gabrielclarke05 pic.twitter.com/Vrm071Zkd1

The FA extended Tuchel’s initial 18-month contract to the end of Euro 2028 last year. He was hired to be the ultimate “finisher”, focusing solely on the first team rather than all the other jobs as a figurehead for the elite pathway that Gareth Southgate filled. His job was specifically to get England over the line after two defeats in Euros finals, a World Cup semi and quarter.

Everyone deserves one tournament’s grace and he has gone farther than anyone bar Sir Alf, Sir Bobby and Sir Gareth. But when you talk so much about your players being brave, you also need to demonstrate your own courage. And clinging on for dear life, even if it worked in different circumstances against lesser opposition, is pusillanimous. Scaloni’s substitutions won the game for Argentina, Tuchel’s lost it for England.

Gutted. Tonight wasn't the result we all hoped for, but this England team has given it their all.The passion and energy they've shown representing the badge has made us all proud.

Gutted. England, you gave it everything and we are all so proud of you.Thank you to everyone on and off the pitch, for an incredible tournament. The fight and belief you have shown has inspired us all. The most complete England team in a tournament. Hold your heads high 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿 W https://t.co/PDBNEsuVfS

12% - Average possession between Anthony Gordon's opener and Lautaro Martínez's winner (55th to 92nd minute):12% - 🏴󠁧󠁢󠁥󠁮󠁧󠁿 England88% - 🇦🇷 ArgentinaTrapped. pic.twitter.com/J5S9wlHCpx

2 - There are just two occasions of a team scoring first in the semi-final but not reaching the final of the FIFA World Cup in the 21st century – both of them are England (vs Croatia in 2018 and Argentina in 2026).Familiar. pic.twitter.com/X645w5FQLk

“We are disappointed. We got so close but we got too passive after we scored and conceded a lot of chances. We could not turn possession around and juts conceded so, so many chances, crosses and shots.

“We were so, so close but we couldn’t keep the level up after we scored.

“I also made offensive substitutions in the last games to try to help the players. We decided to go to a back five because the gaps were too large but they kept crossing and crossing. We went to a back five to close the gaps in the air and win headers. We concedes so many crosses and chances after our goal so we tried to help. But the responsibility is on the coach and if it doesn’t go well it is easy to say it is wrong.

“Yeah [we wanted to go for a second goal] but it doesn’t help when you can’t get the ball. I didn’t think an offensive substitution [at that point] would help. We got more and more passive. We couldn’t keep balls, we couldn’t win balls. It was not a structural problem after the goal. We changed nothing [in the immediate aftermath] but I understand these are the discussions and millions of coaches after the game know better.

“We can discuss this [whether they invited pressure] but I have to make a decision. I have to analyse what I see on the pitch and take responsibility.”

[Any regrets?] “Not at the moment. The team gave everything and we were very, very close. We deserved to be 1-0 up. We played one of our better matches, maybe the best match [of the tournament] but we couldn’t bring it over the line. No regrets.

[Did we ever see the Thomas Tuchel England you wanted to see?] “I don’t have an answer for that question. I don’t know what it means. I think we saw the mentality throughout the matches. We played strong teams in the group, a lot of miles, a lot of travelling, we played at altitude, with 10 men, in the heat and we were very, very close. It is not the time to analyse the tournament. We went very close but lost a crucial match.”

In the celebrations on the final whistle, Argentina fans threw on a banner proclaiming: “Las Malvinas son Argentinas” declaring that the Falkland Islands will remain theirs. This win will potentially renew their demands to claim the Falklands as their own.

We will see if Fifa applies its regulations and fines the AFA.

Tuchel played his hand. He wanted to hang on. We were hanging on against Norway and Mexico but they haven’t got the same quality as Argentina.

He played his cards very early and it’s backfired. We were all praising him in previous games, now we will go the other way. He could have put more pace on. The players were physically and mentally done once Argentina got that first goal.

“Gutted for the boys, everyone. The team, the staff, the fans. We played a good game for the majority of it. Once we went 1-0 up we tried to hold on but at this level you can’t.

“The lads have given everything. Running, sweat, blood tears, whatever. So to fall short like we did today is just gutting.

“We struggled to get pressure on the ball [after scoring]. Whether it was them putting more men forward or us not being able to match them man for man it was juts wave after wave. Lads were putting blocks in but it wasn’t enough.

“The boys are always ready. The message after we went ahead was to go and get another one but when they got their two goals we tried to find something but we couldn’t get back into it.

“We had a lot of good moments, good matches in this tournament. Another semi-final. We keep knocking on the door. We need to find that missing piece in the final part of the tournament. We’re just missing that final piece.”

Wayne Rooney: “We go one-nil up and we go five at the back, we go six at the back. If you allow Argentina to come on to you, you’re in trouble. We surrendered. If you let players of that quality have the ball, you’re in trouble.”

Joe Hart: “Gareth Southgate will be sat at home, watching, and he was criticised for big games when England had the lead, I don’t see anything has changed. For Thomas Tuchel… that’s him saying he didn’t believe in his team and couldn’t land any more punches on Argentina.”

Micah Richards: “Tactically we all thought he got it wrong with his changes.”

Tonight the changes didn’t work. Argentina deserved their victory. Sadly, it’s deja vu for England when the moment of truth arrived.

They froze after scoring. As Joe Hart says, they went back to what they did against Mexico and Norway and packed the defence but Argentina are not Norway or Mexico. They have an indomitable spirit, experience and some great players. You can sit back against Mexico and Norway. You can’t withstand a 30-minute siege against a team like Argentina.

England have lost a third World Cup semi-final. Argentina were the better team. They took more risks, made positive substitutions and found the attacking incisiveness that England lacked all game, apart from when setting up Gordon’s goal.

England pump a long ball up to Dan Burn and Argentina knock it clear. It has come to this.

Rashford gets to the byline on the left and cuts back a cross that Argentina clear. Then Bellingham brings down Messi and Montiel taunts Bellingham when the free-kick was awarded.

England are huffing and puffing, pumping long throws and crosses into the box but without the precision they need.

You can’t say Argentina have not deserved it for their response. They have shown why they are world champions.

I’m afraid England have been sitting ducks ever since they scored.

Rashford and Toney are on for Spence and Stones as England have a throw on the right.

England 1 Argentina 2 (Lautaro Martinez) Gonzalez slides a pass inside from the left to Mac Allister who flays a shot into the foot of the right post. Spence slides into knock it away from Messi but Messi races out to the right to retrieve it and arc over a right-foot cross that Lautaro Martinez creeps between Konsa and Stones to bury with a forceful header.

Lautaro Martinez wins it for Argentina in stoppage time 🇦🇷 pic.twitter.com/7DQKq2DIhk

Bellingham tries to relieve the siege with a long solo run up the left but can’t keep it going.

Nine minutes of stoppage time are signalled after Stones receives treatment for a head injury sustained in a collision with his keeper. He has to go off for a minute.

The chances were coming. Tuchel made his big decisions, but the crosses kept coming into the box. It’s not a great strike for me. The keeper has to save it. Now it’s an awkward one tactically. Tuchel will have to make another change.

England have been under the cosh for the past 20 minutes and have switched to a packed defence. Can they switch back and regain some momentum.

England have got four centre-backs on the pitch. Tuchel has to re-balance this. His changes to try to see the game out were understandable, although they ultimately haven’t worked. England will take extra-time at the moment, because Argentina have the momentum.

England 1 Argentina 1 (Fernandez) Messi takes the corner short to De Paul who gives it back to the captain. Messi then drills a pass to Enzo Fernandez 22 yards out, right of centre. England are happy to soak up the pressure Argentina are trying to exert and attempt to hit them on the break but it’s nerve-racking.

Argentina send on De Paul, Otamendi and Montiel for Simeone, Molina and Lisandro Martinez.

Can England now stop Messi? This could be his last 25 minutes in international football and he is trying everything.

Argentina are coming. You can feel it as well as see it.

For then first time all day, Buenos Aires fell silent as Anthony Gordon put England in front.

Pickford saves England’s lead with a one-handed, scooped save from Gonzalez’s header, the sub artfully picked out by Messi’s cross. Is it too soon for Dan Burn and five at the back?

Fine pass from Kane opens up Argentina on England’s right and James feeds a pass across the 18-yard line to Rice. His attempted daisycutter of a shot turns into a pea-roller and is an easy save for Emi Martinez.

Stones stops the substitute’s first touch, a header back across goal, from reaching Alvarez with a crucial header.

Fernandez fires a shot over the bar after Argentina’s most sustained spell of possession of the match.

Even Victoria Beckham was up celebrating that England goal. She’d stayed in her seat against Norway, but was up hugging husband David when Gordon’s shot at the back post hit the back of the net.

Colossal sense of catharsis as Anthony Gordon, the calmest man in the building, propels England towards the World Cup final with an almost nonchalant finish from Morgan Rogers’ exquisite cross.

Argentina do not panic when behind and have started to play with more fluency as England let them have the ball. Kane shows remarkable composure in his own box to shield the ball from two attackers and see it into safe harbour.

The two wingers combine brilliantly! How long have we wanted more England attackers to come to the party in this World Cup? England needed something else tonight and they’ve got it big style.

Argentina were on the verge of equalising when a mistake allows Simeone to storm down the right and into the box. Just as he was about to pull the trigger on a shot, Spence hared back to slide in and knock the ball behind for a corner. Amazing.

The two sides of Romero, an amazingly insouciant touch in the box a split-second before Gordon as about to steal the ball off him and a turn away from fanger. Then, moments later, he grabs Bellingham round the waist to stop him driving past and is belatedly booked.

England defend the corner taken by Messi short so that he can be played back in up the byline. Bellingham stands his ground and Messi cannot wriggle past him, finding only the brick wall of Bellingham’s torso.

That’s how to deal with Messi. Body in the way first, keep contact, steer him away from trouble. Superb from Bellingham.

Emi Martinez dwells on the ball, trying to invite the press. England stand off and eventually the keeper launches it. Pickford gets down low to beat it away.  Alvarez beats Spence to the rebound and has another go, smashing it into the side netting via a faint deflection.

The biggest cheer of the half here in the Fan Fest arena at the Palermo came when Messi was fouled by Elliot Anderson, prompting the 10,000 fans here to jump up and down to let no-one in doubt that they are English. Otherwise, it has been fairly muted. Perhaps they have been a bit embarrassed about the lack of cards. It suggests England are so far holding their own.

Rice, with one leg of his shorts hitched up, kicks off. No changes. The referee insists the kick-off be taken again as Anthony Gordon had crossed the line before the whistle. Pickford pumps a 70-yard pass up the middle, looking for Kane. Romero wins the header.

We wanted to be the aggressor, we wanted to be on the front foot and get after them – to show there was no inferiority complex and that we’re here to play in their half.

I think it worked until the water break. After that it was a bit more difficult for us to get the ball – they dropped Messi and Enzo [Fernandez] outside our block and got some possession. But it’s still absolutely OK, and the evolution for us is to take this dominance and turn it into bigger chances.

The more time we spend in their half, the more tired they’ll get.

A cagey half, which is to be expected given the stakes. I’ve been impressed with England. They’ve looked dangerous without creating any clear chances. Argentina have created nothing so far.

What’s encouraging is England look like they belong out there, going head-to-head with the world champions, and Elliot Anderson has been outstanding.

This has been the opposite of what the rest of the tournament has been for us. In previous matches there have been a lot of average performances and our two best players have shone. This time, England have played well as a collective while the two best players have been quiet. Bellingham and Kane might have their moment in the second half.

Too many fouls, too many stoppages by design. No shots on target. Both teams are scrapping but there has been very little quality. The foul count is 19, seven by England, 12 by Argentina.

Three shots on goal, none of them on target, just about sums up that first half. Both sides have had a player booked and Kane, Messi and Bellingham have all gone to the referee at the break. This has been scrappy and ill-tempered, but also pretty absorbing.

England work the free-kick to Gordon whose cross is deflected over the bar for a corner but the referee blows for half-time before it can be taken. Clive Thomas, at the age of 90, nods approvingly.

The signal goes up for three minutes of stoppage time, the absolute minimum.

Paredes is penalised for dangerous play for raising his studs in a challenge on James.

It is compelling to watch but not particularly elevating. Romero makes a good tackle on Bellingham. When he is in control of his emotions, he is a fine defender.

England have not made much from their dozen free-kicks. More haste less speed.

The referee sees Lisandro Martinez pulling Rogers’ shirt and finally acts.

FINALLY, this referee has remembered he can also book the players in the navy blue. Lisandro Martinez: yellow.

It’s become cat-and-mouse with Argentina getting more of the ball in the last ten minutes. The one worry is Messi is just starting to take possession much more with some great touches. He hasn’t hurt England yet, though. The hydration break stopped some momentum again, but Tuchel will be pleased overall so far.

Argentina defend the free-kick staunchly as Anderson cannot get above Tagliafico then Messi breaks, hangs on to the ball with sumptuous skill, beating Kane, Spence and Rice before Anderson launches himself at the No10 to stop him. Argentina surround the referee with the distaste of someone who had heard a guest break wind in front of the King. Eventually they get on with the free-kick. Romero next foul is surely a booking. That foul on Bellingham would have been a yellow in the Premier League

James is penalised for a foul on Mac Allister down the right which looked like a foul on the England right-back. Tuchel auditions for the Ministry of Silly Walks in his foot-stomping ire on the touchline.

Pickford scuffs a left-foot clearance and Alvarez is well positioned to pounce on it but he miscontrols his pass to Messi who responds in kind.

First hydration break. No doubt Argentina are trying to wind England up. Noticeable and positive that both Rice and Bellingham have responded by laughing at their tormentors. Eleven fouls in 24 minutes.

There wasn’t an awful lot of football in that first quarter of the game. It’s just been very, very scrappy. Neither set of players seem particularly happy with the referee, which probably tells you he’s done a decent job so far.

Spence stays tight to Simeone after an Argentina free-kick is taken short. The left-back closes the gap and the cross rebounds off his shins for a corner. Pickford tries to push Simeone away as they wait for the corner as the winger tries to block him. The ref tells them to stop messing around and then blows once the corner is launched as Simeone wrapped his arms around the keeper.

After a couple of decent thrusts down the left, England’s right flank gets weaving with Rogers finding the overlapping James whose cross is smothered by Argentina.

The referee Ismail Elfath is really being tested in these early stages. Chiefly by Argentina. Paredes is after Bellingham. Every challenge on Messi is claimed as a foul by Argentina.

It’s clear this is going to be a huge test of the England players’ ability to concentrate on their plan, and to not get drawn into the emotion of the game. On top of that, Argentina are a good side and they are probing for weaknesses. They have come out very aggressively.

England have started well. They look confident and dangerous – more than Argentina. Argentina want to make this a dark arts game. They are better than us at that ‘nasty’ stuff. We can’t get involved in any of that.

Wonderful tackle by Paredes on Bellingham instigates an Argentina counter during which Messi claims he was fouled. He wasn’t but that doesn’t stop him waving his arms around in exasperation. England try to attack up the left only to be stopped by a foul on Gordon. From the quickly taken free-kick, Spence makes great headway down the left, beating Molina again only for Fernandez to race back and force him off the ball and into touch.

Somebody is going to boil over here. Paredes and Fernandez are constantly looking to goad England’s players, much to the chagrin of Thomas Tuchel and captain Harry Kane, who seems to be urging US referee Ismail Elfath to take closer control.

Excellent combination between Gordon and Spence down the England left gets the better of Molina. He cuts in, takes a touch too many which takes him into five Argentina defenders who swarm round him with recovery runs. Sheer weight of numbers push him back.

A foul on Rogers ends with a drop ball after Anderson was deemed to have hurt Paredes in a firm but not illegal tackle. The ref is going to have to get a card out soon. Tuchel is going garrity with Scaloni and the level of diving by Argentina. The most naked example of that is when Simeone picks up the ball having been caught offside and Pickford bumps him as he tries to get it back. Simeone goes down as if kneecapped.

Gordon chases a long pass down the left that has too much on it but he gets there in the nick of time but can’t find Rogers’ run down the inside-left channel. Emi Martinez gathers.

Anderson buys a third free-kick off Simeone with excellent positioning but, after Kane is tackled by Paredes, he fouls Fernandez in this tit for tat start.

Rogers drives at Tagliafico, reaches the byline and whips over a cross that Argentina head behind for a corner. Rice goes long. Simeone wrestles Stones over but the referee waves play on until a high boot clips Messi.

Spence makes good progress down the left, riding a tackle before a forceful sliding tackle wins the ball back. England regroup and Simeone fouls Anderson .

Bellingham and Paredes were mouthing off at each other after that Fernandez foul on Anderson. Messi then had a go at Bellingham too. Feisty start!

England continue to press high and there’s a stand-off between the players with the gobby Paredes in Bellingham’s face after Messi is not given a free-kick when Anderson holds his shoulder. Fernandez retaliates with a chop down Anderson’s shin. Messi, trying to referee the game, gets up in the referee’s grill.

Argentine fans drown out the God Save The King with their own song. England fans respond in kind with the Argentinian anthem. This is a real World Cup knockout tie - and nothing even the stadium address system can do about it. It’s electric in the stadium. I was in Dallas yesterday for the first semi-final and the mood was nothing like as fierce as this.

Argentina kick off, attacking from right to left. Messi, alone in the centre-circle, rolls it to Paredes who works it all the way back to Emi Martinez. England press high and force a throw-in.

England give the ball to the centre-backs who sweep it across the back four. Mac Allister tries to disrupt the spell of possession with a shove.

Michael Buffer’s “champions of the worrrrrrld” billing for England feels a touch generous given it was 60 years ago. I have watched him perform his master of ceremonies shtick in several sports and he barely changes a word. Still, it has earned him, at a conservative estimate, £300m.

God Save the King goes first and is resoundingly booed by the Argentina fans. England supporters respond by whistling and jeering through Himno Nacional Argentino.

A sign? American TV is making a thing of how the England team bus overtook the Argentina team bus, which was snarled up in traffic, on its way to the stadium earlier. Looks like they found a quicker route.

England fans are certainly outnumbered inside the Atlanta stadium, with pockets of Argentina fans in among the main England sections. It has all seem good-natured so far, so let’s hope that lasts.

Once we had the draw, we knew the possible way, these are the games that you dream of to get to where you’re trying to reach, not only reach, don’t forget, but win.

They are specialists [full-backs] and deserve to play. Reece has the quality to play at the very highest level and shows no nerves. The speed of Djed, and he was very good in the last two games from the bench, and I think we need to get our full-backs forward to protect. That’s why.

We need to play with freedom, also for the mindset, we should be ready for the fight, calm and collected to give them some problems to solve. We’re up for it.

Lionel Scaloni has given a nod to the historic rivalry of this fixture by picking Diego Simeone’s son in his starting line-up. Giuliano Simeone is given his first appearance since the final group stage match against Jordan, when Argentina had already sealed a place in the knockouts. His father was famously on the end of David Beckham’s boot 28 years ago in Saint-Étienne. This just adds to the feeling there will be some flashpoint in the next few hours in Atlanta.

While Thomas Tuchel has changed his backline, Argentina are playing the same back four from the team that defeated Switzerland. Simeone is the only change, coming in for Rodrigo De Paul. This could mean a change of system for the world champions – if Enzo Fernández drifts to the right, they could look very 4-4-2.

It doesn’t get much bigger, the biggest competition in football against the holders.

We know what Argentina are about. We’ve got a game plan we’re ready to set up against.

The manager’s made a decision. Morgan is an incredible player, he’s fast, strong and can come inside. He’s a player we’re all looking forward to playing with.

I’m back to normal. I’m 100 per cent, fully refreshed and ready to go.

The team will shock a few people because of Djed Spence’s inclusion. What I like about Thomas Tuchel’s approach is that keeping the same team would be a much easier decision, particularly in a game of this magnitude.

The manager is consistent in selecting players for specialist roles. Just as he called up Dan Burn in case England needed to revert to a back five (as they did against Mexico), the reason he likes Spence is for situations requiring one-on-one defending. Tuchel clearly believes Spence’s pace is a vital attribute in this situation, even if facing Lionel Messi is the ultimate challenge. This is the whole point of Spence being in the squad. If England win, the same logic would suggest he would be asked to mark Lamine Yamal in the final.

From that perspective, maybe we should not be so shocked by the choice.

Morgan Rogers comes in because Tuchel simply could not go with Madueke again. He was poor against Norway, and Saka has not looked fully fit all tournament. Saka is still a good option from the bench.

Overall, Rogers has played well whenever he’s been given a chance, so he’s gone with the man showing form.

This is the first game where England are playing a team who will expect to have as much of, if not more, of the ball than us. Every other game has followed the same pattern, certainly at the start, where England were considered the higher level team and tried to go after the opponent.

That’s not necessarily going to be the case here because we are clearly not favourites, and Argentina will look to apply all the early pressure. Argentina have the edge coming into the match because they are a team that has shown in recent international tournaments that they know how to get over the line.

England deserve to be at this stage, but the question remains: do we have the capacity to do what is habitual for nations like Argentina – finding a way when it matters most in the latter stages of a World Cup?

This is a night for bravery, which means getting on the ball and controlling the game. That could be achieved against every other opponent in this tournament so far, even if some of the victories have been flawed. Doing so against Argentina requires another layer of courage.
